RTX 4090 48 GiB · FP8 · vLLM 0.19.0 · 2026-08-17

Qwen3.6 vs Qwen3.8 湖仓推理 Benchmark

18 道固定题：可执行 SQL、隐藏测试 Python、编码化故障诊断；四组原生 thinking/non-thinking 对比
汇总分类方法SQLPython故障
选型结论
默认推理选择 Qwen3.8-27B-FP8。普通请求保持 non-thinking；复杂 SQL、Python 与故障分析按请求启用 reasoning_effort=low。它在两种模式下均显著高于 Qwen3.6-35B-A3B-FP8，且 low-thinking 18 题无截断、无空 final。Qwen3.6 虽生成更快，但 thinking 无低强度预算控制，本轮 8 题耗尽 4096 tokens，不能作为主要企业湖仓推理模型。

方法与边界

  • 两模型均使用 ModelScope 官方 FP8、同一 RTX 4090、同一固定 digest vLLM 0.19.0，CPU offload 为 0。
  • 每组使用相同题目、最大 4096 输出 tokens 和 seed=42；non-thinking 与 thinking 分别使用官方推荐采样参数。
  • Qwen3.6 只有 thinking 开关；Qwen3.8 使用 reasoning_effort=low，两者不是相同强度的隐藏推理预算。
  • SQL 在 SQLite 内存库执行并精确比较结果；Python 在无网络、只读、非 root、128 MiB 容器执行；故障分析从固定 cause/action codes 选择并惩罚错误操作。
  • 本集合用于本服务器湖仓工程选型，不替代大型公开 benchmark 或 48 小时压力测试。

复杂 SQL逐题证据

cdc_latest_liveQwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking 0.0% · Qwen3.6 FP8 · thinking 100.0% · Qwen3.8 FP8 · non-thinking 0.0% · Qwen3.8 FP8 · thinking-low 0.0%
SQLite table cdc(id TEXT, event_time INT, seq INT, op TEXT, value TEXT) contains change events. Return the latest non-deleted current row for every id as (id, value), ordered by id. A delete is effective only when it is the latest event. Break equal event_time ties by larger seq. Return one SQL code block only.

Qwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking

0.0% · 3.4s · stop
```sql
WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT
        id,
        value,
        ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY id ORDER BY event_time DESC, seq DESC) AS rn
    FROM cdc
),
latest AS (
    SELECT
        id,
        op,
        value
    FROM ranked
    WHERE rn = 1
)
SELECT
    l.id,
    CASE WHEN l.op = 'D' THEN NULL ELSE l.value END AS value
FROM latest l
ORDER BY l.id;
```

```sql
WITH ranked AS (

  FROM cdc

SELECT id, value
FROM ranked
WHERE rn = 1 AND op <> 'D'
ORDER BY id;
```
reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Understand the User's Problem:**
   - Input: SQLite table `cdc(id TEXT, event_time INT, seq INT, op TEXT, value TEXT)`
   - `op` likely represents operations like 'I' (insert), 'U' (update), 'D' (delete), etc. But the problem says "A delete is effective only when it is the latest event." This implies we need to handle deletes carefully.
   - Goal: Return the latest non-deleted current row for every `id` as `(id, value)`, ordered by `id`.
   - Tie-breaking: Equal `event_time` ties are broken by larger `seq`.
   - Output: One SQL code block only.

2.  **Key Requirements & Constraints:**
   - Table: `cdc(id TEXT, event_time INT, seq INT, op TEXT, value TEXT)`
   - For each `id`, find the latest event based on `(event_time DESC, seq DESC)`.
   - If the latest event is a delete (`op = 'D'`), then that `id` is considered deleted and should NOT appear in the output.
   - If the latest event is not a delete, return that `id` and its `value`.
   - Order the result by `id`.
   - SQLite only.

```sql
WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT id, value, op,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY id ORDER BY event_time DESC, seq DESC) AS rn
    FROM cdc
)
SELECT id, value
FROM ranked
WHERE rn = 1 AND op != 'DELETE'
ORDER BY id;
```
reasoning
Let me analyze this problem step by step.

We have a table `cdc` with columns:
- `id` TEXT
- `event_time` INT
- `seq` INT
- `op` TEXT
- `value` TEXT

We need to find the latest non-deleted current row for every `id`.

Key rules:
1. For each `id`, we need to find the latest event (ordered by `event_time` DESC, then `seq` DESC for ties).
2. A delete is effective only when it is the latest event for that `id`.
3. We want rows where the latest event is NOT a delete.
4. Return `(id, value)` ordered by `id`.

So the approach:
1. For each `id`, find the row with the maximum `event_time`, and among those, the maximum `seq`.
2. If that row's `op` is not a delete (i.e., `op != 'DELETE'` or similar), include it.

Wait, what's the delete op value? The problem says "A delete is effective only when it is the latest event." It doesn't explicitly state what the op value is for a delete. Common conventions would be 'DELETE', 'D', or 'delete'. Let me think about this...

The problem says `op TEXT` - it's a text field. The most common convention in CDC (Change Data Capture) systems would be 'DELETE' or 'D'. Since the problem doesn't specify, I'll assume 'DELETE' as it's the most standard. Actually, let me reconsider - it could be 'D' as well.

Looking at the problem again: "A delete is effective only when it is the latest event." This implies that `op` can be 'DELETE' (or similar) to indicate a deletion. I'll go with 'DELETE' as the most common convention.

Approach using window functions:

```sql
WITH ranked AS (
    SELECT id, value, op,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY id ORDER BY event_time DESC, seq DESC) AS rn
    FROM cdc
)
SELECT id, value
FROM ranked
WHERE rn = 1 AND op != 'DELETE'
ORDER BY id;
```

This should work:
1. `ROW_NUMBER()` partitions by `id` and orders by `event_time DESC, seq DESC` to get the latest event per id.
2. We filter to only the latest event (`rn = 1`).
3. We exclude deletes (`op != 'DELETE'`).
4. We order by `id`.

This is clean and correct. Let me verify the logic:
- For each id, we pick the single latest event (by event_time, then seq as tiebreaker).
- If that latest event is a DELETE, we exclude that id entirely.
- If it's an INSERT or UPDATE, we include it with its value.

This matches the requirements perfectly.

```sql
SELECT
    customer_id,
    status,
    effective_at AS valid_from,
    LEAD(effective_at) OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY effective_at) AS valid_to
FROM changes
ORDER BY customer_id, valid_from;
```
reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Understand the User's Request:**
   - **Input Table:** `customer_id TEXT, effective_at TEXT, status TEXT`
   - **Type of Data:** SCD (Slowly Changing Dimension) Type 2 changes
   - **Desired Output:** `customer_id, status, valid_from, valid_to`
   - **Logic for `valid_to`:** Next `effective_at` for that customer, or `NULL` if it's the current/latest record
   - **Ordering:** `customer_id`, `valid_from`
   - **Output Format:** One SQL code block only

2.  **Identify Key SQL Concepts Needed:**
   - Window functions to find the next `effective_at` per customer
   - `LEAD()` window function is perfect for this
   - Partition by `customer_id`, order by `effective_at`
   - Handle `NULL` for the last record (which `LEAD()` naturally does)
   - Rename columns appropriately: `effective_at` -> `valid_from`, next `effective_at` -> `valid_to`

3.  **Draft the SQL Query:**
   ```sql
   SELECT
       customer_id,
       status,
       effective_at AS valid_from,
       LEAD(effective_at) OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY effective_at) AS valid_to
   FROM your_table_name
   ORDER BY customer_id, valid_from;
   ```

4.  **Refine & Check:**
   - Does it match the required output columns? Yes: `customer_id, status, valid_from, valid_to`
   - Does it handle SCD Type 2 correctly? Yes, `LEAD` gets the next effective date. If it's the latest, `LEAD` returns `NULL`, which matches the requirement.
   - Is it ordered correctly? Yes, `ORDER BY customer_id, valid_from`
   - SQLite compatible? Yes, window functions are supported in SQLite 3.25.0+
   - Table name? The prompt says "SQLite table changes(...) contains SCD changes." I'll assume the table is named `changes` or I should use a placeholder. I'll use `changes` as implied, or just write the query assuming the table is named `changes`. The prompt says "SQLite table changes(...)", so the table name is likely `changes`.
   - Let's verify column names: `customer_id`, `effective_at`, `status`
   - Output columns: `customer_id`, `status`, `valid_from`, `valid_to`
   - I'll alias `effective_at` to `valid_from` and use `LEAD(effective_at)` for `valid_to`.

   Draft:
   ```sql
   SELECT
       customer_id,
       status,
       effective_at AS valid_from,
       LEAD(effective_at) OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY effective_at) AS valid_to
   FROM changes
   ORDER BY customer_id, valid_from;
   ```

5.  **Edge Cases & Considerations:**
   - What if there are duplicate `effective_at` dates for the same customer? SCD Type 2 usually has unique effective dates per customer, but if not, `LEAD` still works based on the order. The prompt doesn't specify tie-breaking, so default `ORDER BY effective_at` is fine.
   - SQLite's `LEAD` function: Available since 3.25.0. Standard in modern SQLite.
   - The prompt says "Return one SQL code block only." I will strictly follow that.
   - I'll make sure the table name is `changes` as implied. If not, I can use a CTE or just assume `changes`. I'll stick with `changes`.

   Let's double-check the exact wording: "SQLite table changes(customer_id TEXT, effective_at TEXT, status TEXT) contains SCD changes."
   This implies the table is named `changes`.

   Query:
   ```sql
   SELECT
       customer_id,
       status,
       effective_at AS valid_from,
       LEAD(effective_at) OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY effective_at) AS valid_to
   FROM changes
   ORDER BY customer_id, valid_from;
   ```

   Is there any need to handle `NULL` explicitly? `LEAD` returns `NULL` for the last row, which matches "or NULL". So it's fine.
   Ordering: `ORDER BY customer_id, valid_from` matches requirement.
   Columns: `customer_id, status, valid_from, valid_to` matches requirement.

Qwen3.8 FP8 · thinking-low

100.0% · 15.4s · stop

```sql
SELECT
    customer_id,
    status,
    effective_at AS valid_from,
    LEAD(effective_at) OVER (
        PARTITION BY customer_id
        ORDER BY effective_at
    ) AS valid_to
FROM changes
ORDER BY customer_id, effective_at;
```
reasoning
The user wants to transform an SCD (Slowly Changing Dimension) table into a bitemporal-like view with valid_from and valid_to columns.

Given:
- Table: `changes`
- Columns: `customer_id TEXT`, `effective_at TEXT`, `status TEXT`

The logic is:
- `valid_from` = `effective_at` for the current row
- `valid_to` = the next `effective_at` for the same `customer_id` (ordered by `effective_at`), or NULL if it's the last one

This is a classic "gaps and islands" or "next value" problem, which can be solved with the `LEAD()` window function.

```sql
SELECT
    customer_id,
    status,
    effective_at AS valid_from,
    LEAD(effective_at) OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY effective_at) AS valid_to
FROM changes
ORDER BY customer_id, effective_at;
```

This should work perfectly. `LEAD()` gives the next row's value within the partition, and returns NULL when there's no next row.

sessionize_eventsQwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking 100.0% · Qwen3.6 FP8 · thinking 0.0% · Qwen3.8 FP8 · non-thinking 100.0% · Qwen3.8 FP8 · thinking-low 100.0%
SQLite table events(user_id TEXT, minute INT) contains event timestamps in minutes. A new session starts when the gap from the previous event is greater than 30 minutes. Return (user_id,session_no,start_minute,end_minute,event_count), with session_no starting at 1 per user, ordered by user_id, session_no. Return one SQL code block only.

Qwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking

100.0% · 2.2s · stop
```sql
WITH ordered_events AS (
    SELECT
        user_id,
        minute,
        LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) AS prev_minute
    FROM events
),
session_boundaries AS (
    SELECT
        user_id,
        minute,
        CASE
            WHEN prev_minute IS NULL THEN 1
            WHEN (minute - prev_minute) > 30 THEN 1
            ELSE 0
        END AS is_new_session
    FROM ordered_events
),
session_groups AS (
    SELECT
        user_id,
        minute,
        SUM(is_new_session) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute ROWS UNBOUNDED PRECEDING) AS session_no
    FROM session_boundaries
)
SELECT
    user_id,
    session_no,
    MIN(minute) AS start_minute,
    MAX(minute) AS end_minute,
    COUNT(*) AS event_count
FROM session_groups
GROUP BY user_id, session_no
ORDER BY user_id, session_no;
```

reasoning
Here's a thinking process that leads to the suggested SQL query:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The objective is to group events into sessions for each user based on a time gap threshold (30 minutes). The output needs specific columns: `user_id`, `session_no`, `start_minute`, `end_minute`, and `event_count`. The results must be ordered by `user_id` and `session_no`.

2.  **Analyze the Input:**
    *   Table: `events`
    *   Columns: `user_id` (TEXT), `minute` (INT)
    *   Logic: A new session starts if `current_minute - previous_minute > 30`.

3.  **Step-by-Step Logic Construction:**

    *   **Step 1: Sort the data.**
        To calculate gaps between "previous" and "current" events, the data must be ordered by `user_id` and `minute`.

    *   **Step 2: Identify Session Boundaries (Flagging).**
        I need to compare the current row's minute with the previous row's minute for the same user.
        *   Window Function: `LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute)`
        *   Condition: `minute - LAG(minute) > 30` (or `IS NULL` for the first event of a user).
        *   If this condition is true, it's a new session (flag = 1), otherwise (flag = 0).

    *   **Step 3: Assign Session Numbers.**
        I need a running sum of the flags generated in Step 2.
        *   Window Function: `SUM(flag) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute)`
        *   This cumulative sum will act as the `session_no`.

    *   **Step 4: Aggregate the data.**
        Once each event is assigned a `session_no`, I need to group by `user_id` and `session_no` to find the start, end, and count.
        *   `MIN(minute)` -> `start_minute`
        *   `MAX(minute)` -> `end_minute`
        *   `COUNT(*)` -> `event_count`

4.  **Drafting the SQL (Mental or Scratchpad):**

    *   *CTE 1 (Flags):*
        ```sql
        WITH RankedEvents AS (
            SELECT
                user_id,
                minute,
                CASE
                    WHEN LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) IS NULL THEN 1
                    WHEN minute - LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) > 30 THEN 1
                    ELSE 0
                END as is_new_session
            FROM events
        )
        ```
        *Self-Correction:* Actually, it's cleaner to calculate the gap directly or just handle the null case.
        Let's refine the logic inside the window function or a subquery.
        `LAG(minute)` is the previous minute.
        Gap = `minute - LAG(minute)`.
        If `LAG` is null (first row), gap is effectively infinity (or handled by logic), so new session.
        If `Gap > 30`, new session.

    *   *CTE 2 (Session IDs):*
        ```sql
        Sessionized AS (
            SELECT
                user_id,
                minute,
                SUM(CASE WHEN is_new_session = 1 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)
                    OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) as session_no
            FROM RankedEvents
        )
        ```

    *   *Final Select:*
        ```sql
        SELECT
            user_id,
            session_no,
            MIN(minute) as start_minute,
            MAX(minute) as end_minute,
            COUNT(*) as event_count
        FROM Sessionized
        GROUP BY user_id, session_no
        ORDER BY user_id, session_no;
        ```

5.  **Refining the Logic (Optimization/Simplification):**

    Can I do this in fewer steps?
    Maybe combine the flagging and summing?
    Actually, standard SQL requires the flag to be calculated before the sum can be used as a window function (though some engines allow nesting, it's safer/clearer to separate or use the logic inside the sum).

    Let's look at the gap calculation again.
    `minute - LAG(minute) OVER (...) > 30`
    For the first row, `LAG` returns NULL. `NULL > 30` is unknown/false. So we need to handle the NULL case.
    `COALESCE(LAG(minute)..., minute - 31)`? No, that's hacky.
    `CASE WHEN LAG(...) IS NULL OR minute - LAG(...) > 30 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END`.

    Let's write the CTE structure clearly.

    **Draft 2:**
    ```sql
    WITH EventFlags AS (
        SELECT
            user_id,
            minute,
            CASE
                WHEN LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) IS NULL THEN 1
                WHEN minute - LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) > 30 THEN 1
                ELSE 0
            END as is_new_session
        FROM events
    ),
    SessionedEvents AS (
        SELECT
            user_id,
            minute,
            SUM(is_new_session) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) as session_no
        FROM EventFlags
    )
    SELECT
        user_id,
        session_no,
        MIN(minute) as start_minute,
        MAX(minute) as end_minute,
        COUNT(*) as event_count
    FROM SessionedEvents
    GROUP BY user_id, session_no
    ORDER BY user_id, session_no;
    ```

6.  **Edge Case Check:**
    *   *Empty table:* Works (returns empty set).
    *   *Single event:* `LAG` is null -> `is_new_session` = 1 -> `session_no` = 1. Grouping works.
    *   *Multiple events, no gap:* `LAG` exists, diff <= 30 -> `is_new_session` = 0. `SUM` stays same. Grouping works.
    *   *Multiple events, big gap:* `LAG` exists, diff > 30 -> `is_new_session` = 1. `SUM` increments. Grouping works.
    *   *Ordering:* The prompt asks for `session_no` starting at 1. The cumulative sum does exactly this.

7.  **SQLite Specifics:**
    *   SQLite supports Window Functions (since 3.25.0). This is standard modern SQL.
    *   `LAG` is supported.
    *   `SUM` window function is supported.

8.  **Alternative Approach (Self-Join):**
    Before window functions were ubiquitous, people used self-joins.
    `LEFT JOIN events e2 ON e.user_id = e2.user_id AND e2.minute < e.minute`
    Then find `MAX(e2.minute)`.
    Check gap.
    This is much slower ($O(N^2)$ or worse depending on indexing) and harder to read. Window functions are the correct tool here.

9.  **Final Polish:**
    The prompt asks for "one SQL code block only".
    I will structure it using CTEs for readability, or a single query with subqueries. CTEs are generally preferred for readability in complex logic, but a single query is also fine. I'll stick to CTEs as it's cleaner.

    Let's double check the column names requested: `user_id`, `session_no`, `start_minute`, `end_minute`, `event_count`.
    My draft has these.

    Let's check the condition: "gap from the previous event is greater than 30 minutes".
    Math: `current - previous > 30`.
    Example: Prev=10, Curr=41. Gap=31. New session.
    Example: Prev=10, Curr=40. Gap=30. Same session.
    Logic holds.

    One small detail: `minute` is INT.
    Is it possible for `minute` to be negative? Unlikely for timestamps, but logic holds regardless.

    Let's write the final code.

    ```sql
    WITH FlaggedEvents AS (
        SELECT
            user_id,
            minute,
            CASE
                WHEN LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) IS NULL THEN 1
                WHEN minute - LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) > 30 THEN 1
                ELSE 0
            END AS is_new_session
        FROM events
    ),
    SessionedEvents AS (
        SELECT
            user_id,
            minute,
            SUM(is_new_session) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) AS session_no
        FROM FlaggedEvents
    )
    SELECT
        user_id,
        session_no,
        MIN(minute) AS start_minute,
        MAX(minute) AS end_minute,
        COUNT(*) AS event_count
    FROM SessionedEvents
    GROUP BY user_id, session_no
    ORDER BY user_id, session_no;
    ```

    Can I condense this?
    Yes.
    ```sql
    SELECT
        user_id,
        SUM(CASE WHEN minute - prev_minute > 30 OR prev_minute IS NULL THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) as session_no,
        MIN(minute) as start_minute,
        MAX(minute) as end_minute,
        COUNT(*) as event_count
    FROM (
        SELECT
            user_id,
            minute,
            LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) as prev_minute
        FROM events
    )
    GROUP BY user_id, session_no -- Wait, you can't group by a window function alias in standard SQL easily without a subquery or CTE.
    ```
    Actually, in the subquery approach:
    1. Inner subquery calculates `prev_minute`.
    2. Outer query calculates `session_no` using `SUM(CASE...) OVER(...)`.
    3. But to group by `session_no`, `session_no` must be in the `GROUP BY`. If `session_no` is a window function, it's computed *after* aggregation in standard SQL execution order, or rather, window functions apply to the result set. You cannot use a window function in a `GROUP BY` clause directly in the same SELECT list usually, or at least it's non-standard/complex.
    *Correction:* Window functions are applied *after* `GROUP BY`. So you cannot group by the result of a window function in the same query level.
    Therefore, the CTE approach (or nested subqueries) is necessary to materialize the `session_no` before grouping.

    The CTE approach I drafted in step 5/9 is the standard, correct way to handle this in SQL.

    **Final check of the Prompt Requirements:**
    - Table: `events`
    - Columns: `user_id`, `minute`
    - Logic: Gap > 30 mins -> new session.
    - Output: `user_id`, `session_no` (starts 1), `start_minute`, `end_minute`, `event_count`.
    - Order: `user_id`, `session_no`.
    - Format: One SQL code block.

    The logic `minute - LAG(minute) > 30` handles the gap.
    Handling the first row (NULL LAG) is crucial. `IS NULL` check handles it.
    `SUM(... OVER ...)` handles the session numbering.
    `GROUP BY` handles the aggregation.

    One optimization:
    Instead of two CTEs, I can use one CTE to flag, and then select from it.
    Or even:
    ```sql
    WITH CTE AS (
        SELECT
            user_id,
            minute,
            SUM(CASE WHEN LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) IS NULL
                     OR minute - LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) > 30
                THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)
            OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) as session_no
        FROM events
    )
    SELECT user_id, session_no, MIN(minute) as start_minute, MAX(minute) as end_minute, COUNT(*) as event_count
    FROM CTE
    GROUP BY user_id, session_no
    ORDER BY user_id, session_no;
    ```
    Wait, can I put a window function inside a window function like that?
    `SUM( CASE WHEN LAG(...) ... ) OVER (...)`
    Yes, SQLite supports nested window functions (calculating the inner window function first to produce a value, then aggregating that value with the outer window function).
    However, `LAG` is a window function. `SUM` is an aggregate/window function.
    Inside the `CASE`, `LAG` is evaluated. The result (0 or 1) is then passed to the outer `SUM` window function.
    This is valid in modern SQLite.

    Let's verify syntax: `LAG` returns a value. `CASE` returns 0 or 1. `SUM` aggregates those 0/1s cumulatively.
    This is valid.

    However, for maximum compatibility and readability, the two-step CTE (Flag -> Sum) is safer and easier to debug, though the single CTE is more concise. I will provide the concise but readable version.

    Let's refine the single CTE version for the final output.

    ```sql
    WITH SessionFlags AS (
        SELECT
            user_id,
            minute,
            SUM(CASE
                WHEN LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) IS NULL THEN 1
                WHEN minute - LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) > 30 THEN 1
                ELSE 0
            END) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) AS session_no
        FROM events
    )
    SELECT
        user_id,
        session_no,
        MIN(minute) AS start_minute,
        MAX(minute) AS end_minute,
        COUNT(*) AS event_count
    FROM SessionFlags
    GROUP BY user_id, session_no
    ORDER BY user_id, session_no;
    ```
    This looks solid.

    Wait, is `LAG` allowed inside `SUM` window function arguments?
    Yes. The inner window function (`LAG`) is computed first for every row. Then the outer window function (`SUM`) processes the resulting column.

    Let's double check the `IS NULL` logic.
    Row 1: `LAG` is NULL. `CASE` returns 1. `SUM` (cumulative) becomes 1. Correct.
    Row 2 (gap <= 30): `LAG` is val. `val - val` <= 30. `CASE` returns 0. `SUM` stays 1. Correct.
    Row 3 (gap > 30): `LAG` is val. `val - val` > 30. `CASE` returns 1. `SUM` becomes 2. Correct.

    Resulting columns: `user_id`, `session_no`, `start_minute`, `end_minute`, `event_count`.
    Ordering: `user_id`, `session_no`.

    Looks perfect.

    2. Outer query: Calculate `session_no` based on gap, then aggregate.

    Actually, doing the aggregation *after* calculating session_no requires grouping.
    If I calculate `session_no` in the outer query as a window function, I can't group by it in the same select.
    So:
    1. Subquery/CTE: Calculate `session_no` (Window function).
    2. Main Query: Group by `user_id`, `session_no` to get Min/Max/Count.

                ELSE 0

        MAX(minute) AS end_minute,
        COUNT(*) AS event_count

    ```
    This is the most robust standard SQL solution.

reasoning
Let me think through this step by step.

We have a table `events(user_id TEXT, minute INT)` where we need to identify sessions. A new session starts when the gap from the previous event is greater than 30 minutes.

The approach:
1. For each event, look at the previous event's minute for the same user.
2. If the gap is > 30 minutes (or it's the first event), mark it as a new session start.
3. Use a running sum of these "new session" flags to assign session numbers.
4. Then group by user_id and session_no to get start_minute, end_minute, event_count.

Let me write this using window functions:

```sql
WITH ordered_events AS (
    SELECT
        user_id,
        minute,
        LAG(minute) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) AS prev_minute
    FROM events
),
session_flags AS (
    SELECT
        user_id,
        minute,
        CASE
            WHEN prev_minute IS NULL THEN 1
            WHEN minute - prev_minute > 30 THEN 1
            ELSE 0
        END AS is_new_session
    FROM ordered_events
),
numbered_sessions AS (
    SELECT
        user_id,
        minute,
        SUM(is_new_session) OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY minute) AS session_no
    FROM session_flags
)
SELECT
    user_id,
    session_no,
    MIN(minute) AS start_minute,
    MAX(minute) AS end_minute,
    COUNT(*) AS event_count
FROM numbered_sessions
GROUP BY user_id, session_no
ORDER BY user_id, session_no;
```

This should work. Let me verify the logic:
- `LAG(minute)` gets the previous event's minute for the same user (ordered by minute).
- If there's no previous event (first one), it's a new session.
- If the gap is > 30 minutes, it's a new session.
- Running sum of the new session flags gives us the session number.
- Then we aggregate to get the session boundaries and count.

This looks correct. The output should be one SQL code block only.

SQLite table daily(day TEXT, revenue INT) has one row for each calendar day. Return each day and the inclusive three-row rolling revenue (current day and two preceding days), ordered by day. Return one SQL code block only.

```sql
SELECT
    d.day,
    (
        SELECT SUM(d2.revenue)
        FROM daily d2
        WHERE d2.day <= d.day
          AND d2.day >= DATE(d.day, '-2 days')
    ) AS rolling_revenue
FROM daily d
ORDER BY d.day;
```

SELECT
    d1.day,
    SUM(d2.revenue) AS rolling_revenue
FROM daily d1
JOIN daily d2
    ON d2.day >= DATE(d1.day, '-2 days')
    AND d2.day <= d1.day
GROUP BY d1.day
ORDER BY d1.day;
```

recursive_hierarchyQwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking 0.0% · Qwen3.6 FP8 · thinking 0.0% · Qwen3.8 FP8 · non-thinking 100.0% · Qwen3.8 FP8 · thinking-low 100.0%
SQLite table nodes(id TEXT,parent_id TEXT,amount INT) is a forest. Return (root_id,total_amount) for each root, including all descendant amounts, ordered by root_id. Return one SQL code block only.

Qwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking

0.0% · 1.7s · stop
```sql
WITH RECURSIVE descendant_tree AS (
    SELECT id, parent_id, amount
    FROM nodes
    WHERE parent_id IS NULL
    UNION ALL
    SELECT n.id, n.parent_id, n.amount
    FROM nodes n
    INNER JOIN descendant_tree dt ON n.parent_id = dt.id
),
root_totals AS (
    SELECT
        root_id,
        SUM(amount) AS total_amount
    FROM (
        SELECT
            CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE (
                SELECT d.id FROM descendant_tree d WHERE d.id = descendant_tree.id AND d.parent_id IS NULL
            ) END AS root_id,
            amount
        FROM descendant_tree
    ) sub
    GROUP BY root_id
)
SELECT root_id, total_amount
FROM root_totals
ORDER BY root_id;
```

Qwen3.6 FP8 · thinking

0.0% · 36.7s · length
未返回 final content
reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Understand the User's Request:**
   - **Input Table:** `nodes(id TEXT, parent_id TEXT, amount INT)`
   - **Structure:** It's a forest (a collection of trees). `parent_id` is `NULL` for roots.
   - **Output:** `(root_id, total_amount)` for each root, where `total_amount` includes the root's amount plus all descendants' amounts.
   - **Ordering:** Ordered by `root_id`.
   - **Output Format:** One SQL code block only.

2.  **Identify Key Challenges:**
   - SQLite doesn't have native recursive CTE support in very old versions, but it has supported recursive CTEs since version 3.8.3 (2014). I'll assume modern SQLite with recursive CTE support, as it's standard now.
   - Need to traverse from each node up to its root, or from each root down to its leaves. Traversing up to find the root is usually easier for grouping.
   - Once we have `(node_id, root_id)`, we can join back to the original table to sum amounts per root.
   - Alternatively, traverse down from each root, but that requires knowing roots first.
   - Let's go with the "find root for each node" approach using a recursive CTE.

3.  **Design the Recursive CTE:**
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE root_finder AS (
       -- Base case: start with all nodes
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount,
              CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END as current_id
       FROM nodes
       UNION ALL
       -- Recursive step: move up the tree
       SELECT rf.id, rf.parent_id, rf.amount,
              CASE WHEN n.parent_id IS NULL THEN n.id ELSE n.parent_id END
       FROM root_finder rf
       JOIN nodes n ON rf.current_id = n.id
   )
   ```
   Wait, this is slightly flawed. The recursive step should just follow the parent chain until it hits a root. Let's refine:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE root_finder AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id,
              CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END as root_id
       FROM nodes
       UNION ALL
       SELECT rf.id, n.parent_id,
              CASE WHEN n.parent_id IS NULL THEN n.id ELSE n.root_id END
       FROM root_finder rf
       JOIN nodes n ON rf.parent_id = n.id
   )
   ```
   Actually, a simpler and more standard approach for finding roots:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE tree AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount,
              CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END as root_id
       FROM nodes
       UNION ALL
       SELECT t.id, n.parent_id, t.amount,
              CASE WHEN n.parent_id IS NULL THEN n.id ELSE t.root_id END
       FROM tree t
       JOIN nodes n ON t.parent_id = n.id
   )
   ```
   Let's trace this:
   - Base case: For each node, if parent is null, root is itself. Else, root is parent.
   - Recursive step: Join on `t.parent_id = n.id`. This moves one step up.
   - If `n.parent_id` is null, we've reached the root, so `root_id` becomes `n.id`.
   - Otherwise, `root_id` stays as `t.root_id`.
   - This works, but it might be inefficient or have infinite recursion if there are cycles (but it's a forest/tree, so no cycles).
   - Actually, a more robust way is to just keep walking up until `parent_id` is null:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE path AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount,
              CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END as root_id
       FROM nodes
       UNION ALL
       SELECT p.id, n.parent_id, p.amount,
              CASE WHEN n.parent_id IS NULL THEN n.id ELSE p.root_id END
       FROM path p
       JOIN nodes n ON p.parent_id = n.id
   )
   ```
   Wait, this still has a flaw: `p.root_id` might be overwritten incorrectly if not careful. Let's think differently.
   Standard recursive CTE to find root:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE find_root AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id,
              CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END as root_id
       FROM nodes
       UNION ALL
       SELECT fr.id, n.parent_id,
              CASE WHEN n.parent_id IS NULL THEN n.id ELSE fr.root_id END
       FROM find_root fr
       JOIN nodes n ON fr.parent_id = n.id
   )
   ```
   Actually, the standard way is:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE tree AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount,
              CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END as root_id
       FROM nodes
       UNION ALL
       SELECT t.id, n.parent_id, t.amount,
              CASE WHEN n.parent_id IS NULL THEN n.id ELSE t.root_id END
       FROM tree t
       JOIN nodes n ON t.parent_id = n.id
   )
   SELECT id, root_id, amount FROM tree WHERE parent_id IS NULL;
   ```
   This gets the root for each node. Then we can sum amounts grouped by root_id.
   Let's test this logic mentally:
   - Node A (root): parent_id=NULL. Base case: root_id = A.
   - Node B (child of A): parent_id=A. Base case: root_id = A.
   - Node C (child of B): parent_id=B. Base case: root_id = B.
   - Recursive step for C: joins on parent_id=B -> n.id=B. n.parent_id=A. Since n.parent_id is not null, root_id stays B.
   - Next recursive step for C: joins on parent_id=A -> n.id=A. n.parent_id=NULL. root_id becomes A.
   - Next recursive step for C: joins on parent_id=NULL -> no match (assuming no node has id=NULL). Stops.
   - So C ends up with root_id=A. Correct.
   - But wait, the base case already sets root_id to parent_id for non-roots. So for C, base case gives B. Then recursion moves to A. So it works.
   - However, this generates intermediate rows for each step up the tree. That's fine.
   - To get the final root_id for each node, we can just take the last row for each `id`, or filter where `parent_id IS NULL` in the CTE result.
   - Actually, in the CTE `tree`, when `parent_id IS NULL`, that row represents the node at the root level for that chain. So `SELECT id, root_id, amount FROM tree WHERE parent_id IS NULL` gives exactly `(node_id, root_id, amount)` for each node's root.
   - Let's verify: For node C, the last row in `tree` will have `parent_id IS NULL` and `root_id = A`. Correct.
   - So:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE tree AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount,
              CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END as root_id
       FROM nodes
       UNION ALL
       SELECT t.id, n.parent_id, t.amount,
              CASE WHEN n.parent_id IS NULL THEN n.id ELSE t.root_id END
       FROM tree t
       JOIN nodes n ON t.parent_id = n.id
   )
   SELECT root_id, SUM(amount) as total_amount
   FROM tree
   WHERE parent_id IS NULL
   GROUP BY root_id
   ORDER BY root_id;
   ```
   Wait, is `root_id` guaranteed to be the actual root when `parent_id IS NULL`? Yes, because the recursion stops when `parent_id IS NULL`, and at that point `root_id` holds the ultimate root.
   Let's double check with a simple example:
   Nodes: (1, NULL, 10), (2, 1, 20), (3, 2, 30)
   Base:
   1: id=1, parent=NULL, amount=10, root=1
   2: id=2, parent=1, amount=20, root=1
   3: id=3, parent=2, amount=30, root=2
   Recursion step 1:
   Join tree t with nodes n on t.parent_id = n.id
   t=1: parent=NULL -> no join
   t=2: parent=1 -> n.id=1, n.parent=NULL -> new row: id=2, parent=NULL, amount=20, root=1
   t=3: parent=2 -> n.id=2, n.parent=1 -> new row: id=3, parent=1, amount=30, root=2
   Recursion step 2:
   t=2 (from step 1): parent=NULL -> no join
   t=3 (from step 1): parent=1 -> n.id=1, n.parent=NULL -> new row: id=3, parent=NULL, amount=30, root=1
   Now filter `parent_id IS NULL`:
   From base: (1, NULL, 10, root=1)
   From step 1: (2, NULL, 20, root=1)
   From step 2: (3, NULL, 30, root=1)
   Group by root_id: root=1, sum=10+20+30=60. Correct.

   This works perfectly.

4.  **Consider Edge Cases & Optimizations:**
   - What if `parent_id` references a non-existent node? The problem says it's a forest, so we assume valid tree structure.
   - What if there are cycles? Problem says forest, so no cycles.
   - Is there a simpler way? Some people use `WITH RECURSIVE` to find roots by just walking up until `parent_id IS NULL`, but the above is standard.
   - Actually, a more concise recursive CTE for finding roots:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE root_path AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount,
              CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END as root_id
       FROM nodes
       UNION ALL
       SELECT rp.id, n.parent_id, rp.amount,
              CASE WHEN n.parent_id IS NULL THEN n.id ELSE rp.root_id END
       FROM root_path rp
       JOIN nodes n ON rp.parent_id = n.id
   )
   SELECT root_id, SUM(amount) as total_amount
   FROM root_path
   WHERE parent_id IS NULL
   GROUP BY root_id
   ORDER BY root_id;
   ```
   This is clean.

   Wait, SQLite's recursive CTE requires the recursive term to not reference itself in a way that causes infinite loops, but this is fine.
   One minor thing: `root_id` in the `SELECT` might be ambiguous if not aliased, but it's fine.
   Let's verify column names: `id`, `parent_id`, `amount`.
   Output: `(root_id, total_amount)`.
   Order: `ORDER BY root_id`.

   Could there be a case where `parent_id` is NULL for roots, but the base case `CASE WHEN parent_id IS NULL THEN id ELSE parent_id END` sets `root_id` to `id` for roots. That's correct.
   For non-roots, it sets `root_id` to `parent_id`. Then recursion moves up.
   This is correct.

   Alternative approach: Bottom-up aggregation? Not possible in SQL without recursion.
   Another approach: Top-down from roots.
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE tree AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount, id as root_id, 0 as depth
       FROM nodes WHERE parent_id IS NULL
       UNION ALL
       SELECT n.id, n.parent_id, n.amount, t.root_id, t.depth + 1
       FROM tree t
       JOIN nodes n ON n.parent_id = t.id
   )
   SELECT root_id, SUM(amount) as total_amount
   FROM tree
   GROUP BY root_id
   ORDER BY root_id;
   ```
   This is actually much cleaner and more efficient! It starts from roots and walks down. Since it's a forest, roots are easily identified. No need for `CASE` logic in recursion. It naturally propagates `root_id` down the tree.
   Let's trace:
   Base: roots have `root_id = id`.
   Recursive: children get `root_id` from parent.
   Finally, group by `root_id` and sum.
   This avoids the `WHERE parent_id IS NULL` filter trick and intermediate rows. It's standard and robust.
   I'll use this top-down approach.

   Check: Does it handle disconnected nodes correctly? Yes, starts from all roots.
   Check: Does it handle single-node trees? Yes, base case includes them.
   Check: Output format: `(root_id, total_amount)`.
   Check: Ordering: `ORDER BY root_id`.
   Check: SQLite compatibility: Recursive CTEs are supported. `SUM(amount)` works.

   Let's write it out:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE tree AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount, id AS root_id
       FROM nodes
       WHERE parent_id IS NULL
       UNION ALL
       SELECT n.id, n.parent_id, n.amount, t.root_id
       FROM tree t
       JOIN nodes n ON n.parent_id = t.id
   )
   SELECT root_id, SUM(amount) AS total_amount
   FROM tree
   GROUP BY root_id
   ORDER BY root_id;
   ```

   Wait, is there any risk of duplicate counting? No, each node is visited exactly once per tree it belongs to. Since it's a forest (each node has at most one parent), each node belongs to exactly one tree. So each node appears exactly once in the `tree` CTE. Summing is safe.

   One minor point: The problem says "including all descendant amounts". This implies the root's own amount is included. My query includes the root in the base case, so its amount is included in the sum. Correct.

   Let's double check the exact column names and types: `id TEXT`, `parent_id TEXT`, `amount INT`.
   The query uses them correctly.

   Is there any SQLite-specific quirk? Recursive CTEs in SQLite require `WITH RECURSIVE` or just `WITH` (SQLite allows omitting `RECURSIVE` keyword, but it's safer to include it or just use `WITH`). I'll stick with `WITH RECURSIVE`.
   Also, SQLite's `UNION ALL` is fine.

   One thing: The problem says "Return (root_id,total_amount) for each root, including all descendant amounts, ordered by root_id."
   The query does exactly that.

   Let's consider if there's a simpler way without recursive CTE? Not really, SQL requires recursion for tree traversal.
   Could use a correlated subquery with a recursive function? Not standard.
   Recursive CTE is the standard solution.

   Final check of the query:
   ```sql
   WITH RECURSIVE tree AS (
       SELECT id, parent_id, amount, id AS root_id
       FROM nodes
       WHERE parent_id IS NULL
       UNION ALL
       SELECT n.id, n.parent_id, n.amount, t.root_id
       FROM tree t
       JOIN nodes n ON n.parent_id = t.id
   )
   SELECT root_id, SUM(amount) AS total_amount
   FROM tree
   GROUP BY root_id
   ORDER BY root_id;
   ```
   This looks solid.

   Wait, what if `parent_id` can be empty string `''` instead of `NULL`? The problem says `parent_id TEXT`, and typically in such problems, roots have `NULL` or empty string. I'll assume `NULL` as it's standard. If it's empty string, I could use `WHERE parent_id IS NULL OR parent_id = ''`, but I'll stick to `NULL` as specified in typical tree problems. The prompt says "is a forest", implying standard tree structure.

   One more check: Does SQLite allow `WITH RECURSIVE`? Yes.
   Does it support `SUM`? Yes.
   Order by `root_id`? Yes.
   Output format: one SQL code block only.

   I will produce exactly that.
   I'll also add a comment if needed, but the prompt says "Return one SQL code block only." So I'll just output the code block.

   Let's verify once more with a quick mental run:
   Table:
   id | parent_id | amount
   A  | NULL      | 10
   B  | A         | 20
   C  | B         | 30
   D  | NULL      | 5

   CTE tree:
   Base: A (root=A, amt=10), D (root=D, amt=5)
   Rec1: B joins A -> (B, A, 20, root=A), C joins B -> wait, C's parent is B, but B is in tree now? Yes, recursive CTE processes all rows from previous iteration.
   Actually, standard recursive CTE processes row by row, but logically it expands level by level.
   Iteration 1: B (root=A, 20), C (root=A, 30) [since C joins B, gets B's root_id]
   Iteration 2: No more children.
   Final tree rows: A, D, B, C.
   Group by root_id: A -> 10+20+30=60, D -> 5.
   Correct.

   One subtle point: In SQLite, recursive CTEs evaluate the recursive term against the *entire* result set from the previous iteration, not just newly added rows. This is fine and standard.

```sql
WITH RECURSIVE subtree(root_id, node_id, amount) AS (
    SELECT id, id, amount FROM nodes WHERE parent_id IS NULL
    UNION ALL
    SELECT s.root_id, n.id, n.amount
    FROM subtree s
    JOIN nodes n ON n.parent_id = s.node_id
)
SELECT root_id, SUM(amount) AS total_amount
FROM subtree
GROUP BY root_id
ORDER BY root_id;
```

```sql
WITH RECURSIVE tree(root_id, id, amount) AS (
    SELECT id, id, amount
    FROM nodes
    WHERE parent_id IS NULL
    UNION ALL
    SELECT t.root_id, n.id, n.amount
    FROM nodes n
    JOIN tree t ON n.parent_id = t.id
)
SELECT root_id, SUM(amount) AS total_amount
FROM tree
GROUP BY root_id
ORDER BY root_id;
```
reasoning
The user has a SQLite table `nodes(id TEXT, parent_id TEXT, amount INT)` that represents a forest (collection of trees). They want to find each root node and sum up the amounts of all its descendants (including itself).

In SQLite, I can use a recursive CTE to traverse the tree from each root down to all descendants, then group by root_id and sum the amounts.

A root node is one where `parent_id IS NULL`.

Let me write this:

```sql
WITH RECURSIVE tree(root_id, id, amount) AS (
    -- Base case: root nodes
    SELECT id, id, amount FROM nodes WHERE parent_id IS NULL
    UNION ALL
    -- Recursive case: children
    SELECT t.root_id, n.id, n.amount
    FROM nodes n
    JOIN tree t ON n.parent_id = t.id
)
SELECT root_id, SUM(amount) AS total_amount
FROM tree
GROUP BY root_id
ORDER BY root_id;
```

This should work. The recursive CTE starts with all root nodes (where parent_id IS NULL), then recursively joins to find all descendants. Then we group by root_id and sum the amounts.

funnel_first_orderQwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking 0.0% · Qwen3.6 FP8 · thinking 0.0% · Qwen3.8 FP8 · non-thinking 0.0% · Qwen3.8 FP8 · thinking-low 100.0%
SQLite tables users(user_id TEXT,signup_day TEXT) and orders(user_id TEXT,order_day TEXT) are given. Return one row (users_total,converted_7d). A user converts when their first order is on or after signup_day and no more than 7 days later. Orders before signup do not count and later orders cannot replace an earlier valid post-signup order. Return one SQL code block only.

Qwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking

0.0% · 1.6s · stop
```sql
SELECT
    COUNT(DISTINCT u.user_id) AS users_total,
    COUNT(DISTINCT CASE
        WHEN o.first_order_day IS NOT NULL
        AND o.first_order_day >= u.signup_day
        AND o.first_order_day <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days')
        THEN u.user_id
    END) AS converted_7d
FROM users u
LEFT JOIN (
    SELECT user_id, MIN(order_day) AS first_order_day
    FROM orders
    GROUP BY user_id
) o ON u.user_id = o.user_id;
```

reasoning
Here's a thinking process that leads to the suggested SQL query:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The objective is to calculate two metrics based on two tables (`users` and `orders`):
    *   `users_total`: The total count of users.
    *   `converted_7d`: The count of users who placed their *first* order within 7 days of signing up (inclusive).

2.  **Analyze the Schema:**
    *   `users` table: `user_id` (TEXT), `signup_day` (TEXT).
    *   `orders` table: `user_id` (TEXT), `order_day` (TEXT).
    *   *Note:* The dates are stored as TEXT. I need to assume a format compatible with SQLite date functions (usually 'YYYY-MM-DD').

3.  **Define "Conversion" Logic:**
    *   A user converts if their *first* order is $\ge$ `signup_day` AND $\le$ `signup_day` + 7 days.
    *   "First order" means the minimum `order_day` for that user.
    *   "Orders before signup do not count" implies we only care about orders happening on or after signup.
    *   "Later orders cannot replace an earlier valid post-signup order" is a bit ambiguous but usually implies: If a user orders on day 0 (signup), they converted. If they order on day 10 (before signup) and day 2 (after signup), the day 2 order is the first *post-signup* order? Or is it just the absolute first order?
    *   *Re-reading the prompt carefully:* "Orders before signup do not count". This suggests we filter orders where `order_day` >= `signup_day`. Then, among those valid orders, find the earliest one.
    *   *Alternative interpretation:* "First order" usually means the absolute first order in the table. However, the constraint "Orders before signup do not count" strongly suggests we should ignore pre-signup orders when determining the "first order" for conversion purposes.
    *   *Let's look at the specific constraint:* "no more than 7 days later".
    *   *Refined Logic:*
        1.  Join users and orders.
        2.  Filter orders where `order_day` >= `signup_day`.
        3.  For each user, find the minimum `order_day` from the remaining set (the first valid order).
        4.  Check if this minimum `order_day` is within 7 days of `signup_day`.
        5.  Count total users.
        6.  Count converted users.

4.  **Handling Date Arithmetic in SQLite:**
    *   SQLite has `julianday()` or `strftime()` or `date()` functions.
    *   To check "no more than 7 days later", we can check if `julianday(order_day) - julianday(signup_day) <= 7`.
    *   Or use `date(signup_day, '+7 days')`.

5.  **Step-by-Step Query Construction:**

    *   *Step 1: Get Total Users.*
        `SELECT COUNT(user_id) FROM users`

    *   *Step 2: Identify Converted Users.*
        This is the tricky part. We need to aggregate orders per user.

        *Approach A: Subquery/CTE for First Order*
        ```sql
        WITH UserFirstOrder AS (
            SELECT
                u.user_id,
                u.signup_day,
                MIN(o.order_day) as first_order_day
            FROM users u
            JOIN orders o ON u.user_id = o.user_id
            WHERE o.order_day >= u.signup_day -- Orders before signup don't count
            GROUP BY u.user_id
        )
        SELECT
            COUNT(user_id) as users_total,
            SUM(CASE WHEN julianday(first_order_day) - julianday(signup_day) <= 7 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) as converted_7d
        FROM users u
        LEFT JOIN UserFirstOrder ufo ON u.user_id = ufo.user_id
        -- Wait, this logic is slightly flawed. If a user has NO valid orders, they aren't in UFO.
        -- But the prompt implies "A user converts when their first order is...". If they have no orders, they don't convert.
        ```

        *Let's refine the logic based on the prompt's specific phrasing:*
        "A user converts when their first order is on or after signup_day and no more than 7 days later."

        Does "first order" mean the absolute first order in the `orders` table, or the first order *after* signup?
        Text: "Orders before signup do not count".
        This implies if a user signed up on Jan 1st, and ordered on Dec 31st and Jan 2nd. The Dec 31st order is ignored. The Jan 2nd order becomes the reference point.

        So, the logic is:
        1. Find the earliest `order_day` for a user where `order_day` >= `signup_day`.
        2. If such an order exists and `order_day` <= `signup_day` + 7 days, they are converted.

    *   *Approach B: Aggregating inside the main query*
        We can calculate the metrics in one pass or using a CTE.

        Let's try to calculate the "First Valid Order Date" per user.

        ```sql
        WITH ValidOrders AS (
            SELECT
                o.user_id,
                MIN(o.order_day) as min_order_day
            FROM orders o
            JOIN users u ON o.user_id = u.user_id
            WHERE o.order_day >= u.signup_day
            GROUP BY o.user_id
        )
        ```
        Now, join this back to users to check the date difference and count.

        ```sql
        SELECT
            (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) as users_total,
            COUNT(v.user_id) as converted_7d -- Count users who have a valid order within 7 days
        FROM users u
        LEFT JOIN ValidOrders v ON u.user_id = v.user_id
        WHERE v.min_order_day IS NOT NULL
          AND julianday(v.min_order_day) - julianday(u.signup_day) <= 7
        ```
        *Wait*, the `COUNT(v.user_id)` will only count rows where the condition is met. But we need the total users in the same row.

        Using a subquery for total users is safer.

    *   *Refining the Date Check:*
        `julianday` works well. `date(u.signup_day, '+7 days')` is also readable.
        `v.min_order_day <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days')`

    *   *Handling Edge Cases:*
        *   User with no orders: `v.user_id` is NULL. Condition fails. Not converted. Correct.
        *   User with orders but all before signup: `v.user_id` is NULL (due to WHERE clause in CTE). Not converted. Correct.
        *   User with orders after signup but > 7 days: `v.user_id` exists, but date check fails. Not converted. Correct.
        *   User with orders after signup <= 7 days: `v.user_id` exists, date check passes. Converted. Correct.

6.  **Drafting the SQL:**

    ```sql
    WITH FirstValidOrder AS (
        SELECT
            o.user_id,
            MIN(o.order_day) as first_order_day
        FROM orders o
        INNER JOIN users u ON o.user_id = u.user_id
        WHERE o.order_day >= u.signup_day
        GROUP BY o.user_id
    )
    SELECT
        (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) as users_total,
        COUNT(fvo.user_id) as converted_7d
    FROM users u
    LEFT JOIN FirstValidOrder fvo ON u.user_id = fvo.user_id
    WHERE fvo.first_order_day <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days');
    ```
    *Critique:* The `WHERE` clause filters out users who didn't convert. But `COUNT(fvo.user_id)` counts non-nulls. If I filter in the WHERE clause, I am only looking at converted users.
    Wait, `COUNT(fvo.user_id)` counts non-null values. If I filter `WHERE fvo.first_order_day <= ...`, I am restricting the result set to *only* converted users.
    So `COUNT(fvo.user_id)` would equal the number of converted users.
    BUT, the query returns *one row*.
    If I filter the result set, I might get multiple rows (one per user) or just the count.
    Ah, I need to aggregate.

    *Correction:*
    The structure needs to be:
    1. Select from Users.
    2. Left Join FirstValidOrder.
    3. Filter for conversion condition? No, that would drop non-converted users from the result set if I'm doing a standard SELECT.
    4. But I need `users_total` (count of ALL users) and `converted_7d` (count of subset).

    If I do:
    ```sql
    SELECT
        (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) as users_total,
        COUNT(CASE WHEN fvo.first_order_day <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days') THEN 1 END) as converted_7d
    FROM users u
    LEFT JOIN FirstValidOrder fvo ON u.user_id = fvo.user_id;
    ```
    This looks correct.
    1. `users_total` is a scalar subquery returning total count.
    2. We iterate over all users (via `FROM users u`).
    3. We join the first valid order (if any).
    4. We count how many satisfy the date condition.

    *Wait, is `MIN(o.order_day)` safe with TEXT dates?*
    SQLite compares strings lexicographically. If dates are 'YYYY-MM-DD', lexicographical order is chronological. '2023-01-05' < '2023-01-10'. This works.

    *Is `date(u.signup_day, '+7 days')` safe?*
    Yes, standard SQLite function.

    *Let's check the constraint "Orders before signup do not count".*
    In the CTE `FirstValidOrder`, I used `WHERE o.order_day >= u.signup_day`. This ensures we only look at orders on or after signup.
    Then `MIN(o.order_day)` finds the earliest of these.
    Then we check if that specific order is within 7 days.

    *What if a user has an order on Day 1 and Day 8?*
    CTE finds Day 1. Day 1 <= Day 0 + 7. Converted. Correct.

    *What if a user has an order on Day 8 and Day 10?*
    CTE finds Day 8. Day 8 > Day 0 + 7. Not converted. Correct.

    *What if a user has an order on Day -1 (before signup) and Day 2?*
    CTE filters out Day -1. Finds Day 2. Day 2 <= Day 0 + 7. Converted. Correct.

    *What if a user has an order on Day -1 (before signup) and Day 10?*
    CTE filters out Day -1. Finds Day 10. Day 10 > Day 0 + 7. Not converted. Correct.

    *What if a user has NO orders?*
    CTE returns nothing for this user. LEFT JOIN results in NULL.
    `first_order_day` is NULL.
    Condition `NULL <= ...` is UNKNOWN (false).
    Count is 0. Correct.

7.  **Optimization/Simplification:**
    Do we strictly need a CTE?
    We can do it with a subquery or just a GROUP BY, but CTE is cleaner.
    However, standard SQL often prefers a single pass if possible, though performance isn't the primary constraint here, correctness is.

    Let's try to write it without CTE to be concise, or keep CTE for clarity. CTE is generally preferred for readability in complex logic.

    *Alternative without CTE:*
    ```sql
    SELECT
        (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) as users_total,
        SUM(
            CASE
                WHEN first_order.order_day <= date(users.signup_day, '+7 days') THEN 1
                ELSE 0
            END
        ) as converted_7d
    FROM users
    LEFT JOIN (
        SELECT user_id, MIN(order_day) as order_day
        FROM orders
        GROUP BY user_id
    ) first_order ON users.user_id = first_order.user_id
    -- Wait, the subquery above gets the absolute first order.
    -- We need the first order *after* signup.
    ```
    The subquery `MIN(order_day)` doesn't respect the "signup_day" constraint unless we join it.
    Joining inside the subquery is messy.

    Let's stick to the CTE approach or a correlated subquery approach.

    *Correlated Subquery Approach:*
    For each user, find the min order day >= signup day.
    ```sql
    SELECT
        (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) as users_total,
        SUM(
            CASE
                WHEN (
                    SELECT MIN(order_day)
                    FROM orders o
                    WHERE o.user_id = u.user_id
                      AND o.order_day >= u.signup_day
                ) <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days')
                THEN 1
                ELSE 0
            END
        ) as converted_7d
    FROM users u;
    ```
    This is logically sound and requires no CTE. It handles the "Orders before signup do not count" by filtering inside the subquery. It handles "first order" by taking MIN. It checks the 7-day window.

    *Performance note:* Correlated subqueries can be slow on large datasets, but for a logic puzzle or standard SQL interview question, it's often acceptable. However, the CTE (or a JOIN with aggregation) is usually more performant.

    Let's refine the CTE version to be robust.

    ```sql
    WITH UserFirstOrder AS (
        SELECT
            o.user_id,
            MIN(o.order_day) as first_valid_order_day
        FROM orders o
        JOIN users u ON o.user_id = u.user_id
        WHERE o.order_day >= u.signup_day
        GROUP BY o.user_id
    )
    SELECT
        (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) as users_total,
        COUNT(ufo.first_valid_order_day) as converted_7d -- Wait, COUNT counts non-nulls.
    FROM users u
    LEFT JOIN UserFirstOrder ufo ON u.user_id = ufo.user_id
    WHERE ufo.first_valid_order_day <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days');
    ```
    *ERROR in logic above:* If I put the date check in the `WHERE` clause, I am filtering the result set to *only* converted users.
    If I do `SELECT ... FROM users ... WHERE converted`, I get a list of converted users.
    But I need `users_total` (all users) and `converted_7d` (count).
    If I filter the rows, `users_total` (calculated via subquery) is still correct (it's a scalar), but `COUNT(ufo...)` would be the count of the filtered rows.
    Actually, that *would* work for the converted count, but it's risky if the logic gets complex.

    Better to use `SUM(CASE ...)` or `COUNT(CASE ...)` to keep the row set as "All Users" and flag the conversion status.

    *Revised CTE Logic:*
    1. Calculate total users.
    2. For each user, determine if they converted.

    ```sql
    WITH ValidFirstOrders AS (
        SELECT
            o.user_id,
            MIN(o.order_day) as order_day
        FROM orders o
        JOIN users u ON o.user_id = u.user_id
        WHERE o.order_day >= u.signup_day
        GROUP BY o.user_id
    )
    SELECT
        (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) as users_total,
        COUNT(vfo.order_day) as converted_7d
    FROM users u
    LEFT JOIN ValidFirstOrders vfo ON u.user_id = vfo.user_id
    WHERE vfo.order_day <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days');
    ```
    *Wait, check the WHERE clause again.*
    If I have 100 users. 10 converted.
    The `LEFT JOIN` creates 100 rows.
    The `WHERE` clause filters these 100 rows.
    It keeps the 10 rows where the condition is true.
    It discards the 90 rows where the condition is false (or null).
    Then `COUNT(vfo.order_day)` counts the 10 rows.
    And `(SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users)` returns 100.
    Result: Row with (100, 10).

    *Is this valid SQL?*
    Yes.
    *Is it efficient?*
    It scans users, joins, filters. It works.

    *However*, strictly speaking, filtering in the WHERE clause after a LEFT JOIN acts like an INNER JOIN for the condition.
    If a user has no orders, `vfo.order_day` is NULL. `NULL <= date` is false. Row removed.
    If a user has orders but > 7 days, row removed.
    So yes, this effectively counts the converted users.

    *Alternative (Safer/Cleaner) Logic:*
    Don't filter rows, just count matches.
    ```sql
    WITH ValidFirstOrders AS (
        SELECT
            o.user_id,
            MIN(o.order_day) as order_day
        FROM orders o
        JOIN users u ON o.user_id = u.user_id
        WHERE o.order_day >= u.signup_day
        GROUP BY o.user_id
    )
    SELECT
        (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) as users_total,
        SUM(CASE WHEN vfo.order_day <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days') THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) as converted_7d
    FROM users u

```sql
SELECT
    (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) AS users_total,
    (SELECT COUNT(*)
     FROM users u
     WHERE (
         SELECT MIN(o.order_day)
         FROM orders o
         WHERE o.user_id = u.user_id
           AND o.order_day >= u.signup_day
     ) <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days')
    ) AS converted_7d;
```
reasoning
Let me break down the problem:

1. We have two tables: `users(user_id TEXT, signup_day TEXT)` and `orders(user_id TEXT, order_day TEXT)`
2. We need to return one row with two columns: `users_total` and `converted_7d`
3. A user "converts" when:
   - Their first order is on or after signup_day
   - Their first order is no more than 7 days after signup_day
   - Orders before signup do not count
   - Later orders cannot replace an earlier valid post-signup order

So the logic is:
- `users_total` = COUNT of all users in the users table
- `converted_7d` = COUNT of users where their first order (considering only orders on or after signup_day) is within 7 days of signup_day

Key points:
- "First order" means the earliest order that is on or after signup_day
- If that earliest valid order is within 7 days (inclusive) of signup_day, the user has converted
- Orders before signup are ignored entirely
- "Later orders cannot replace an earlier valid post-signup order" - this just reinforces that we look at the FIRST valid order (on or after signup)

So for each user:
1. Filter orders where order_day >= signup_day
2. Find the MIN(order_day) from those filtered orders
3. Check if that min order_day is within 7 days of signup_day (i.e., order_day <= signup_day + 7 days)

In SQLite, we can use `date(signup_day, '+7 days')` to add 7 days.

Let me write the SQL:

```sql
SELECT
    (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users) AS users_total,
    (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users u
     WHERE EXISTS (
         SELECT 1 FROM orders o
         WHERE o.user_id = u.user_id
         AND o.order_day >= u.signup_day
         AND o.order_day <= date(u.signup_day, '+7 days')
         AND o.order_day = (
             SELECT MIN(o2.order_day)
             FROM orders o2
             WHERE o2.user_id = u.user_id
             AND o2.order_day >= u.signup_day
         )
     )
    ) AS converted_7d
```

Wait, let me simplify. If the first valid order (min order_day where order_day >= signup_day) is within 7 days, then the user converted. So I can just check:

For each user, does there exist an order where:
- order_day >= signup_day
- order_day <= date(signup_day, '+7 days')
- AND that order_day is the minimum among all orders where order_day >= signup_day

Actually, a simpler way: if the MIN of valid orders (>= signup_day) is <= signup_day + 7 days, then converted.

Let me use a cleaner approach:

```sql

- For each user, find the earliest order that is on or after signup_day
- If that earliest order is within 7 days (inclusive) of signup_day, the user converted
- If no such order exists, MIN is NULL, condition is false

This handles all the edge cases correctly. The "later orders cannot replace an earlier valid post-signup order" is naturally handled because we're taking the MIN.

Implement def current_rows(events). Each event is a dict with id, event_time, seq, op, value. Keep the latest by (event_time,seq), omit ids whose latest op is D, and return surviving dicts with only id and value ordered by id. Return Python code only.

```python
def current_rows(events):
    """
    Process events to return the current state of each id.

    Each event is a dict with keys: id, event_time, seq, op, value.
    - Keep the latest event for each id based on (event_time, seq).
    - Omit ids whose latest operation is 'D' (delete).
    - Return surviving dicts with only 'id' and 'value', ordered by id.
    """
    # Group events by id and find the latest one per id
    latest_events = {}

    for event in events:
        eid = event['id']
        event_time = event['event_time']
        seq = event['seq']

        if eid not in latest_events:
            latest_events[eid] = event
        else:
            existing = latest_events[eid]
            existing_key = (existing['event_time'], existing['seq'])
            new_key = (event_time, seq)
            if new_key > existing_key:
                latest_events[eid] = event

    # Filter out deleted ids and build result
    result = []
    for eid, event in latest_events.items():
        if event['op'] != 'D':
            result.append({
                'id': event['id'],
                'value': event['value']
            })

    # Sort by id
    result.sort(key=lambda x: x['id'])

    return result
```

def current_rows(events):
    latest = {}
    for e in events:
        key = e['id']
        ts = (e['event_time'], e['seq'])
        if key not in latest or ts > latest[key][0]:
            latest[key] = (ts, e)
    result = []
    for id_ in sorted(latest.keys()):
        _, e = latest[id_]
        if e['op'] != 'D':
            result.append({'id': id_, 'value': e['value']})
    return result

Implement def stable_toposort(graph). graph maps a node to its dependencies. Return a dependency-first order, using original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready. Raise ValueError for a missing dependency or cycle. Do not mutate the input. Return Python code only.

```python
def stable_toposort(graph):
    """
    Perform a topological sort on the given graph.

    Args:
        graph: A dict mapping nodes to their dependencies (list of nodes).

    Returns:
        A list of nodes in dependency-first order.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If there's a missing dependency or a cycle.
    """
    # Build adjacency and in-degree structures
    # We need to track all nodes, including those that are only dependencies

    # Collect all nodes
    all_nodes = set()
    for node, deps in graph.items():
        all_nodes.add(node)
        for dep in deps:
            all_nodes.add(dep)

    # Check for missing dependencies
    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in all_nodes:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep}")

    # Build reverse adjacency: for each node, which nodes depend on it?
    # Also compute in-degrees
    in_degree = {node: 0 for node in all_nodes}
    # adj[u] contains list of nodes v such that u -> v (u must come before v)
    adj = {node: [] for node in all_nodes}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            # dep must come before node
            adj[dep].append(node)
            in_degree[node] += 1

    # Use a queue-like structure that respects insertion order
    # Start with nodes that have in_degree 0
    # We need to maintain the original insertion order from the graph dict
    # among nodes with in_degree 0

    # To preserve insertion order, we'll use a list as a queue, but we need
    # to process nodes in the order they appear in the original graph when multiple are ready.

    # Let's collect initial zero in-degree nodes in the order they appear in graph keys
    # But also include nodes that are only dependencies and never keys in graph

    # Actually, let's think about this more carefully.
    # The "original dict insertion order" refers to the order of keys in `graph`.
    # When multiple nodes are ready (in_degree == 0), we should pick them in the order
    # they first appeared as keys in the graph dict.

    # Create an ordering based on the graph's key insertion order
    ordered_keys = list(graph.keys())

    # For nodes not in graph.keys() (only appear as dependencies), we need to decide their position.
    # They should be processed after all keys that were inserted before them?
    # Actually, nodes that only appear as dependencies don't have a natural "insertion order"
    # from the graph's perspective. Let's assign them an order based on when they first appear
    # as a dependency, maintaining stability.

    # Better approach: create a total ordering where:
    # - Keys in graph come first, in their insertion order
    # - Nodes only appearing as dependencies come after, in the order they first appear as a dependency

    # Let's build a priority/ordering index for each node
    node_order = {}
    idx = 0
    for node in ordered_keys:
        node_order[node] = idx
        idx += 1

    # For nodes not yet assigned (only appear as dependencies), assign them in order of first appearance
    seen_deps = set()
    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in node_order:
                node_order[dep] = idx
                idx += 1

    # Now use Kahn's algorithm with a min-heap or sorted structure based on node_order
    # But we want stable order, so when multiple nodes have in_degree 0, pick the one
    # with smallest node_order value.

    import heapq

    # Initialize heap with all nodes having in_degree 0
    heap = []
    for node in all_nodes:
        if in_degree[node] == 0:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (node_order[node], node))

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(node)
        for neighbor in adj[node]:
            in_degree[neighbor] -= 1
            if in_degree[neighbor] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (node_order[neighbor], neighbor))

    if len(result) != len(all_nodes):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected in graph")

    return result
```

4.  **Edge Cases & Details:**

```python
import heapq

def stable_toposort(graph):
    # Validate dependencies and build reverse graph
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}
    in_degree = {node: 0 for node in graph}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep}")
            dependents[dep].append(node)
            in_degree[node] += 1

    # Insertion order indices for stability
    order = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph.keys())}

    # Initialize heap with nodes that have no dependencies
    heap = [(order[node], node) for node in graph if in_degree[node] == 0]
    heapq.heapify(heap)

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, u = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(u)
        for v in dependents[u]:
            in_degree[v] -= 1
            if in_degree[v] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (order[v], v))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected in graph")

    return result
```

```python
def stable_toposort(graph):
    """
    Perform a stable topological sort on the given graph.

    Args:
        graph: A dict mapping each node to its list of dependencies.

    Returns:
        A list of nodes in dependency-first order. When multiple nodes are ready,
        they are ordered by their original insertion order in the graph dict.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If a dependency is not present in the graph or if there's a cycle.
    """
    # Make a copy of the graph to avoid mutating input
    # We need to track remaining dependencies for each node
    # and also track which nodes depend on each node (reverse adjacency)

    # Get all nodes in original insertion order
    nodes_in_order = list(graph.keys())
    node_set = set(nodes_in_order)

    # Check for missing dependencies
    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in node_set:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep} not found in graph")

    # Build reverse adjacency: for each node, which nodes depend on it
    dependents = {node: [] for node in nodes_in_order}
    # Track remaining dependency count for each node
    remaining_deps = {}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        remaining_deps[node] = len(deps)
        for dep in deps:
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    # Initialize queue with nodes that have no dependencies, in original insertion order
    from collections import deque

    # Use a list as a priority-like structure based on original insertion order
    # We'll use a simple approach: maintain a list of ready nodes, and pick from them
    # in the order they appear in the original graph

    # Map node to its index in the original order for stable sorting
    node_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(nodes_in_order)}

    # Start with all nodes having 0 dependencies, sorted by original insertion order
    ready = [node for node in nodes_in_order if remaining_deps[node] == 0]
    # ready is already in original insertion order since we iterate nodes_in_order

    result = []

    while ready:
        # Pick the first ready node (which is in original insertion order among ready nodes)
        # But we need to ensure that when new nodes become ready, they are inserted
        # in the correct position relative to other ready nodes

        # Actually, let me think more carefully. The requirement says "using original
        # dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready". This means at each step,
        # among all currently ready nodes, we should pick the one that appears first
        # in the original dict insertion order.

        # So we should always pick the ready node with the smallest original index.
        # Let's use a min-heap or just find the min each time. For simplicity, let's
        # keep ready as a list and always pick the one with smallest node_index.

        # Find the ready node with the smallest original insertion index
        min_idx = float('inf')
        chosen = None
        for node in ready:
            idx = node_index[node]
            if idx < min_idx:
                min_idx = idx
                chosen = node

        result.append(chosen)
        ready.remove(chosen)

        # Now, for each node that depends on chosen, decrement its remaining deps
        for dependent in dependents[chosen]:
            remaining_deps[dependent] -= 1
            if remaining_deps[dependent] == 0:
                ready.append(dependent)

    if len(result) != len(nodes_in_order):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected in graph")

    return result
```

```python
import heapq


def stable_toposort(graph):
    in_degree = {}
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        unique_deps = set(deps)
        in_degree[node] = len(unique_deps)
        for dep in unique_deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep!r}")
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    order_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph)}

    heap = []
    for node in graph:
        if in_degree[node] == 0:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[node], node))

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(node)
        for dependent in dependents[node]:
            in_degree[dependent] -= 1
            if in_degree[dependent] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[dependent], dependent))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected")

    return result
