We have a `graph` that maps a node to its dependencies. So `graph = {A: [B, C], B: [], C: []}` means A depends on B and C.

We need to return a "dependency-first order" - meaning if A depends on B, B comes before A in the result.

Constraints:
1. Use original dict insertion order whenever multiple nodes are ready (i.e., when multiple nodes have all their dependencies satisfied, pick the one that appears first in the original dict's insertion order).
2. Raise ValueError for a missing dependency (a dependency that isn't a key in the graph) or a cycle.
3. Do not mutate the input.

Let me think about the algorithm:

1. First, validate that all dependencies exist in the graph (are keys). If any dependency is not a key in the graph, raise ValueError.
2. Use Kahn's algorithm (topological sort) but with a priority based on the original insertion order.

For Kahn's algorithm:
- Compute in-degree for each node (number of dependencies it has, or rather, number of nodes that depend on it... wait, let me think again).

Actually, let me reconsider. The graph maps a node to its dependencies. So if A depends on B, then B must come before A.

In terms of topological sort:
- Edge from B to A (B must come before A)
- In-degree of A is the number of dependencies A has (i.e., len(graph[A]))
- A node is "ready" when its in-degree is 0 (all dependencies are satisfied)

Wait, let me reconsider the direction. If A depends on B, then B must appear before A. So the edge is B → A. The in-degree of a node is the number of its dependencies (edges pointing to it from its dependencies).

So:
- in_degree[node] = len(graph[node])
- Initially, nodes with in_degree 0 are ready.
- When we process a node, we need to find all nodes that depend on it and decrement their in_degree.

To find "all nodes that depend on it", I need a reverse adjacency list: for each node, which nodes list it as a dependency.

Let me think about the "original dict insertion order" constraint. When multiple nodes are ready, we pick the one that appears first in the original dict's insertion order.

So I need to maintain a priority based on the original insertion order. I can assign each node an index based on its position in the dict's keys, and always pick the ready node with the smallest index.

I can use a sorted structure or simply scan through the ready set each time picking the one with the smallest original index. For efficiency, I could use a heap with the original index as priority.

Let me code this up:

```python
import heapq

def stable_toposort(graph):
    # Build reverse adjacency and in-degrees
    # in_degree[node] = number of dependencies (edges from deps to node)
    in_degree = {node: len(deps) for node, deps in graph.items()}

    # Reverse adjacency: for each dependency, which nodes depend on it
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}
    for node, deps in graph.items():
        for dep in deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep}")
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    # Original insertion order index
    order_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph)}

    # Initialize heap with nodes that have in_degree 0
    heap = []
    for node in graph:
        if in_degree[node] == 0:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[node], node))

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(node)
        for dependent in dependents[node]:
            in_degree[dependent] -= 1
            if in_degree[dependent] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[dependent], dependent))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected")

    return result
```

Wait, I need to be careful about not mutating the input. I'm creating new dicts (`in_degree`, `dependents`, `order_index`) and a new list (`result`), so the input `graph` is not mutated. The `deps` lists are iterated but not modified. Good.

Let me also consider: what if a node has duplicate dependencies? Like `graph = {A: [B, B]}`. The in_degree would be 2, but when we process B, we'd decrement A's in_degree by 1 (since B appears once in dependents[B]). That would be a bug.

Hmm, let me think about this. If `graph = {A: [B, B]}`, then A depends on B (listed twice). The in_degree should probably be 1 (A has 1 unique dependency), or should it be 2?

Actually, I think the safer interpretation is that dependencies might have duplicates, and we should handle them. Let me use a set for dependencies to avoid this issue.

```python
import heapq

def stable_toposort(graph):
    in_degree = {}
    dependents = {node: [] for node in graph}

    for node, deps in graph.items():
        unique_deps = set(deps)
        in_degree[node] = len(unique_deps)
        for dep in unique_deps:
            if dep not in graph:
                raise ValueError(f"Missing dependency: {dep}")
            dependents[dep].append(node)

    order_index = {node: i for i, node in enumerate(graph)}

    heap = []
    for node in graph:
        if in_degree[node] == 0:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[node], node))

    result = []
    while heap:
        _, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        result.append(node)
        for dependent in dependents[node]:
            in_degree[dependent] -= 1
            if in_degree[dependent] == 0:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (order_index[dependent], dependent))

    if len(result) != len(graph):
        raise ValueError("Cycle detected")

    return result
```

This looks good. Let me trace through an example:

`graph = {'A': ['B', 'C'], 'B': [], 'C': []}`

- in_degree: A=2, B=0, C=0
- dependents: A=[], B=['A'], C=['A']
- order_index: A=0, B=1, C=2
- Initial heap: (1, 'B'), (2, 'C')
- Pop B → result = ['B'], decrement A's in_degree to 1
- Pop C → result = ['B', 'C'], decrement A's in_degree to 0, push (0, 'A')
- Pop A → result = ['B', 'C', 'A']

That's correct - dependencies first.

Another example: `graph = {'A': [], 'B': [], 'C': ['A', 'B']}`
- in_degree: A=0, B=0, C=2
- dependents: A=['C'], B=['C'], C=[]
- order_index: A=0, B=1, C=2
- Initial heap: (0, 'A'), (1, 'B')
- Pop A → result = ['A'], C's in_degree → 1
- Pop B → result = ['A', 'B'], C's in_degree → 0, push (2, 'C')
- Pop C → result = ['A', 'B', 'C']

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    """
    Compare two nested dicts with type string leaves.
    Return sorted list of dotted paths for:
    - Missing fields (in expected but not in actual)
    - Extra fields (in actual but not in expected)
    - Type mismatches (type differs between expected and actual)

    For missing/extra nested objects, report their leaf paths.
    """
    results = []

    def _compare(exp, act, prefix=""):
        if exp is None or act is None:
            # If either is None at this point, we might have a mismatch
            # But the problem states leaves are type strings, so we expect both to be dicts until leaves
            return

        # Get all keys from both dicts
        all_keys = set(list(exp.keys()) + list(act.keys()))

        for key in all_keys:
            dotted_key = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key

            if key not in exp:
                # Key is extra in actual
                # Report leaf paths under this key in actual
                _report_leaf_paths(act[key], dotted_key, "extra")
            elif key not in act:
                # Key is missing in actual
                # Report leaf paths under this key in expected
                _report_leaf_paths(exp[key], dotted_key, "missing")
            else:
                # Key exists in both
                exp_val = exp[key]
                act_val = act[key]

                # Check if both are dicts (nested structure)
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    _compare(exp_val, act_val, dotted_key)
                elif isinstance(exp_val, str) and isinstance(act_val, str):
                    # Both are leaves (type strings)
                    if exp_val != act_val:
                        results.append(dotted_key)
                else:
                    # One is a dict, the other is a leaf (or vice versa)
                    # This is a structural mismatch; report leaf paths from both sides
                    # Treat as both missing and extra? Let's think...
                    # If expected has a dict here but actual has a string,
                    # then the expected leaves are missing and actual leaf is extra.
                    if isinstance(exp_val, dict):
                        _report_leaf_paths(exp_val, dotted_key, "missing")
                        results.append(dotted_key)  # The actual value itself is extra
                    elif isinstance(act_val, dict):
                        _report_leaf_paths(act_val, dotted_key, "extra")
                        results.append(dotted_key)  # The expected value itself is missing
                    else:
                        # Both are non-dict, non-string? Shouldn't happen per spec
                        results.append(dotted_key)

    def _report_leaf_paths(node, path_prefix, kind):
        """Report all leaf paths under node with the given kind."""
        if isinstance(node, dict):
            for key, value in node.items():
                child_path = f"{path_prefix}.{key}"
                _report_leaf_paths(value, child_path, kind)
        else:
            # It's a leaf (type string)
            results.append((path_prefix, kind))

    # Wait, the above approach mixes things up. Let me reconsider.
    # The function should return sorted dotted paths. Each path indicates an issue.
    # For missing fields: paths that exist in expected but not in actual (including all sub-paths if it's a nested object)
    # For extra fields: paths that exist in actual but not in expected (including all sub-paths if it's a nested object)
    # For type differences: paths where both exist but types differ

    # Let me redo this more carefully.
    results = []

    def _collect_leaves(d, prefix):
        """Collect all leaf paths from a dict."""
        leaves = []
        for key, value in d.items():
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            if isinstance(value, dict):
                leaves.extend(_collect_leaves(value, p))
            else:
                leaves.append(p)
        return leaves

    def _compare_recursive(exp, act, prefix=""):
        """Recursively compare expected and actual dicts."""
        if not isinstance(exp, dict) or not isinstance(act, dict):
            return

        exp_keys = set(exp.keys())
        act_keys = set(act.keys())

        # Keys only in expected (missing in actual)
        missing_keys = exp_keys - act_keys
        # Keys only in actual (extra in actual)
        extra_keys = act_keys - exp_keys
        # Keys in both
        common_keys = exp_keys & act_keys

        # Handle missing keys
        for key in missing_keys:
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            # Collect all leaf paths under this missing key in expected
            leaves = _collect_leaves(exp[key], p)
            results.extend(leaves)

        # Handle extra keys
        for key in extra_keys:
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else p
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            # Collect all leaf paths under this extra key in actual
            leaves = _collect_leaves(act[key], p)
            results.extend(leaves)

        # Handle common keys
        for key in common_keys:
            p = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            exp_val = exp[key]
            act_val = act[key]

            if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                _compare_recursive(exp_val, act_val, p)
            elif isinstance(exp_val, str) and isinstance(act_val, str):
                if exp_val != act_val:
                    results.append(p)
            else:
                # Structural mismatch: one is dict, other is leaf, or both are different types
                # Report all leaf paths from expected as missing, and all leaf paths from actual as extra
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict):
                    leaves_exp = _collect_leaves(exp_val, p)
                    results.extend(leaves_exp)
                else:
                    results.append(p)  # The leaf itself is considered missing structure

                if isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    leaves_act = _collect_leaves(act_val, p)
                    results.extend(leaves_act)
                else:
                    results.append(p)  # The leaf itself is considered extra structure

    _compare_recursive(expected, actual)

    # Sort and remove duplicates
    unique_results = sorted(set(results))

    return unique_results
```

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    def get_leaves(d, path):
        res = []
        if isinstance(d, dict):
            for k, v in d.items():
                res.extend(get_leaves(v, path + [k]))
        else:
            res.append(".".join(path))
        return res

    def compare(exp, act, path):
        res = []
        # Union of keys to catch missing and extra
        keys = set(list(exp.keys()) + list(act.keys()))

        for k in keys:
            curr_path = path + [k]
            in_exp = k in exp
            in_act = k in act

            if in_exp and in_act:
                e_val = exp[k]
                a_val = act[k]

                if isinstance(e_val, dict) and isinstance(a_val, dict):
                    res.extend(compare(e_val, a_val, curr_path))
                else:
                    # Type mismatch or structure mismatch
                    # If one is dict and other is string, or strings differ
                    if e_val != a_val:
                        # Check if it's a type string mismatch
                        # The prompt implies leaves are type strings.
                        # If we are here, at least one is not a dict (or both are dicts but handled above)
                        # Actually, if both are dicts, we recursed.
                        # So here, at least one is a leaf (string).
                        # If both are strings and differ -> mismatch
                        # If one is string, one is dict -> mismatch
                        # But wait, if e_val is dict and a_val is string, e_val != a_val is True.
                        # But strictly speaking, if e_val is dict, it's not a leaf.
                        # The prompt says "leaves are type strings".
                        # So if we are at a node where one is dict and one is string, it's a structural drift.
                        # Should we report the path or expand?
                        # "A missing/extra nested object reports its leaf paths."
                        # This specific instruction applies to missing/extra keys.
                        # For type mismatch, usually we report the key.
                        # Let's assume reporting the key `curr_path` is correct for mismatches.
                        res.append(".".join(curr_path))

            elif in_exp and not in_act:
                # Missing in actual
                e_val = exp[k]
                if isinstance(e_val, dict):
                    # Report leaf paths of the missing nested object
                    res.extend(get_leaves(e_val, curr_path))
                else:
                    # Missing leaf
                    res.append(".".join(curr_path))

            elif not in_exp and in_act:
                # Extra in actual
                a_val = act[k]
                if isinstance(a_val, dict):
                    # Report leaf paths of the extra nested object
                    res.extend(get_leaves(a_val, curr_path))
                else:
                    # Extra leaf
                    res.append(".".join(curr_path))

        return res

    results = compare(expected, actual, [])
    return sorted(results)
```

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    """
    Compare two nested dicts representing schemas.
    Leaves are type strings.
    Returns a sorted list of dotted paths where:
      - A field is missing in 'actual' (present in 'expected')
      - A field is extra in 'actual' (not present in 'expected')
      - A leaf field has a different type

    For missing/extra nested objects, report all their leaf paths.
    """
    drifts = []

    def _compare(exp, act, prefix):
        # exp and act should both be dicts at this level
        if not isinstance(exp, dict) or not isinstance(act, dict):
            # Both should be leaves (type strings) at this point
            # If one is dict and other is string, that's a type mismatch
            # But per problem, leaves are type strings, so we handle at parent level
            return

        exp_keys = set(exp.keys())
        act_keys = set(act.keys())

        # Missing fields: in expected but not in actual
        for key in exp_keys - act_keys:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            _collect_leaf_paths(exp[key], path, drifts)

        # Extra fields: in actual but not in expected
        for key in act_keys - exp_keys:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            _collect_leaf_paths(act[key], path, drifts)

        # Common keys: compare
        for key in exp_keys & act_keys:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            exp_val = exp[key]
            act_val = act[key]

            if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                _compare(exp_val, act_val, path)
            elif isinstance(exp_val, str) and isinstance(act_val, str):
                if exp_val != act_val:
                    drifts.append(path)
            else:
                # Type mismatch: one is dict, other is string
                # Report the leaf paths
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict):
                    _collect_leaf_paths(exp_val, path, drifts)
                if isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    _collect_leaf_paths(act_val, path, drifts)
                # If one is dict and other is string, we've collected from the dict side
                # The string side doesn't have sub-paths, so just note it's a drift
                # Actually, if one is dict and other is string, it's a structural mismatch.
                # We should report the leaf paths of the dict side.
                # But what about the string side? It's a single leaf.
                # Let's think: if expected has a nested object and actual has a string,
                # then all leaf paths under expected's object are "missing" in actual,
                # and actual's string is an "extra" leaf at that path?
                # Hmm, this is tricky. Let me re-read the problem.

                # "A missing/extra nested object reports its leaf paths."
                # So if expected has a nested object at some path and actual doesn't have it (or has something else),
                # we report the leaf paths of the nested object as missing.
                # Similarly for extra.

                # In this case, since types differ (dict vs string), we treat it as:
                # - All leaves under the dict are missing (if dict is in expected) or extra (if dict is in actual)
                # - The string itself... well, if expected is string and actual is dict, then expected's string is missing? No.

                # Let me simplify: if they're both strings, compare directly.
                # If one is dict and other is string, collect leaf paths from the dict side.
                # This handles the case where structure changed.
                pass

    def _collect_leaf_paths(value, path, out):
        """Collect all leaf paths under value, prefixed with path."""
        if isinstance(value, dict):
            if not value:
                # Empty dict - no leaves
                return
            for k, v in value.items():
                sub_path = f"{path}.{k}" if path else k
                _collect_leaf_paths(v, sub_path, out)
        else:
            # It's a leaf (string)
            out.append(path)

    _compare(expected, actual, "")
    return sorted(drifts)
```

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    results = set()

    def _collect_leaves(d, prefix):
        for k, v in d.items():
            path = f"{prefix}.{k}" if prefix else k
            if isinstance(v, dict):
                _collect_leaves(v, path)
            else:
                results.add(path)

    def _compare(exp, act, prefix):
        for key in exp:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            if key not in act:
                if isinstance(exp[key], dict):
                    _collect_leaves(exp[key], path)
                else:
                    results.add(path)
            else:
                exp_val = exp[key]
                act_val = act[key]
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    _compare(exp_val, act_val, path)
                elif isinstance(exp_val, dict) or isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    results.add(path)
                else:
                    if exp_val != act_val:
                        results.add(path)

        for key in act:
            if key not in exp:
                path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
                if isinstance(act[key], dict):
                    _collect_leaves(act[key], path)
                else:
                    results.add(path)

    _compare(expected, actual, "")
    return sorted(results)
```

We have two nested dicts where leaves are type strings. We need to find:

3. Fields whose type differs

For nested objects, if a key is missing or extra, we report all its leaf paths (not the parent path).

- `expected` and `actual` are nested dicts
- Leaves are type strings (like "int", "str", "float", etc.)
- A value that is a dict means it's a nested object
- A value that is a string means it's a leaf (type annotation)

Algorithm:
1. Walk both dicts simultaneously
2. For each key in expected:
   - If key not in actual: it's missing → report all leaf paths under this key

     - If both are dicts: recurse
     - If both are strings (leaves): compare types, if different report the path

Wait, let me re-read: "Return sorted dotted paths for missing fields, extra fields, or fields whose type differs. A missing/extra nested object reports its leaf paths."

So:
- If a key exists in expected but not in actual, and its value is a dict (nested object), we report all leaf paths under it as "missing"
- If a key exists in actual but not in expected, and its value is a dict, we report all leaf paths under it as "extra"
- If a key exists in both but types differ (both leaves with different type strings), we report that path

```python
def schema_drift(expected, actual):
    results = set()

    def _collect_leaves(d, prefix):
        """Collect all leaf paths in a dict."""
        for k, v in d.items():
            path = f"{prefix}.{k}" if prefix else k
            if isinstance(v, dict):
                _collect_leaves(v, path)
            else:
                results.add(path)

    def _compare(exp, act, prefix):
        for key in exp:
            path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
            if key not in act:
                # Missing field
                if isinstance(exp[key], dict):
                    _collect_leaves(exp[key], path)
                else:
                    results.add(path)
            else:
                exp_val = exp[key]
                act_val = act[key]
                if isinstance(exp_val, dict) and isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    _compare(exp_val, act_val, path)
                elif isinstance(exp_val, dict) or isinstance(act_val, dict):
                    # One is nested, other is leaf - type mismatch
                    results.add(path)
                else:
                    # Both are leaves
                    if exp_val != act_val:
                        results.add(path)

        for key in act:
            if key not in exp:
                path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
                # Extra field
                if isinstance(act[key], dict):
                    _collect_leaves(act[key], path)
                else:
                    results.add(path)

    _compare(expected, actual, "")
    return sorted(results)
```

Let me trace through an example:
- expected = {"a": {"b": "int", "c": "str"}, "d": "float"}
- actual = {"a": {"b": "str", "e": "int"}, "d": "float"}

For key "a": both are dicts, recurse with prefix "a"
  - key "b": in both, both leaves, "int" != "str" → add "a.b"
  - key "c": in exp not in act, leaf → add "a.c"
  - key "e": in act not in exp, leaf → add "a.e"
For key "d": in both, both leaves, "float" == "float" → nothing

Result: ["a.b", "a.c", "a.e"]

Another example:
- expected = {"a": {"b": "int", "c": "str"}}
- actual = {"d": "float"}

For key "a": in exp not in act, it's a dict → collect leaves: "a.b", "a.c"
For key "d": in act not in exp, leaf → add "d"

Result: ["a.b", "a.c", "d"]

This looks correct. Let me also consider edge cases:
- Empty dicts? If expected = {} and actual = {}, no drift.
- What if a value is an empty dict? That would have no leaf paths, so nothing would be reported for it. That seems reasonable.

I think this solution is correct. Let me finalize it.

Implement def bounded_batches(items, max_rows). items is an iterable of (partition,row_count). Preserve order and return a list of batches, each a list of items, whose total rows does not exceed max_rows. An item larger than max_rows must be alone. Reject max_rows <= 0. Qwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking

100.0% · 2.5s · stop
```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_row_count = 0

    for item in items:
        partition, row_count = item

        # If the item itself exceeds max_rows, it must be alone
        if row_count > max_rows:
            # Flush current batch if not empty
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_row_count = 0
            # Add the oversized item as its own batch
            batches.append([item])
        else:
            # Check if adding this item would exceed max_rows
            if current_row_count + row_count > max_rows:
                # Flush current batch
                if current_batch:
                    batches.append(current_batch)
                # Start new batch with this item
                current_batch = [item]
                current_row_count = row_count
            else:
                current_batch.append(item)
                current_row_count += row_count

    # Don't forget the last batch
    if current_batch:
        batches.append(current_batch)

    return batches
```

        *   So the constraint "An item larger than max_rows must be alone" is actually a redundant consequence of the capacity constraint, *unless* the capacity constraint is relaxed for that specific item. But since `row_count > max_rows`, `row_count + x > max_rows` is always true for `x >= 0`.
        *   So, strictly speaking, the "must be alone" rule is automatically satisfied by the sum constraint if we assume row counts are non-negative.
        *   *However*, if the prompt implies that an item > max_rows is allowed to exist in a batch (violating the sum constraint) but *only* if it's the only item, my logic holds. If I allowed it to be combined, it would violate the sum constraint anyway. So the logic is safe.

6.  **Code Structure:**

    ```python
    def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
        if max_rows <= 0:
            raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

        batches = []
        current_batch = []
        current_rows = 0

        for partition, row_count in items:
            # Check if item fits in current batch
            # Note: If row_count > max_rows, it won't fit if current_rows >= 0.
            # But specifically, if current_batch is not empty, we check fit.
            # If current_batch is empty, we just add it (even if it exceeds max_rows).

            if current_batch and current_rows + row_count > max_rows:
                # Flush current batch
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_rows = 0

            current_batch.append((partition, row_count))
            current_rows += row_count

        # Don't forget the last batch
        if current_batch:
            batches.append(current_batch)

        return batches
    ```

7.  **Edge Case Analysis:**
    *   `items` empty: Loop doesn't run. `current_batch` empty. Returns `[]`. Correct.
    *   `max_rows` 0 or negative: Raises ValueError. Correct.
    *   Item exactly `max_rows`: `current_rows + row_count` == `max_rows`. Fits. Correct.
    *   Item > `max_rows`:
        *   If `current_batch` empty: Adds it. `current_rows` becomes > `max_rows`.
        *   Next item: `current_rows + next_row > max_rows` (since `current_rows` already huge).
        *   Flushes the huge batch. Starts new one.
        *   Result: Huge item is alone. Correct.
    *   Order preservation: Iterating `items` sequentially and appending to lists preserves order. Correct.
    *   Single pass: `for` loop iterates once. Correct.

    Only if `current_rows` is negative? No, row counts are counts.

    Item (P, 100) is alone.
    Logic holds.

    *   The check `if current_batch` is slightly redundant if we initialize `current_rows = 0` and check `current_rows + row_count > max_rows`?
        *   If `current_batch` is empty, `current_rows` is 0.
        *   If `row_count <= max_rows`, `0 + row_count <= max_rows` is True. It adds.
        *   If `row_count > max_rows`, `0 + row_count > max_rows` is True. It enters the `if` block.
        *   Inside `if`: Appends empty list? No, `current_batch` is empty. `batches.append([])`. That's bad. We don't want empty batches in the result.
        *   So the check `if current_batch` is necessary to avoid appending empty lists when an oversized item triggers a flush of an empty buffer (though an empty buffer flush is impossible in this logic flow, except maybe if we initialized differently).
        *   Wait, if `current_batch` is empty, `current_rows` is 0.
        *   If we have an oversized item `row_count > max_rows`.
        *   `if current_batch` (False) -> Skip.
        *   Add item. `current_batch` has item. `current_rows` > `max_rows`.
        *   Next item comes. `if current_batch` (True). `current_rows + next > max_rows`.
        *   Flush. `batches.append(current_batch)` (The oversized one).
        *   Reset `current_batch = []`, `current_rows = 0`.
        *   Add next item.
        *   This works perfectly.

    ```python
    def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
        if max_rows <= 0:
            raise ValueError("max_rows must be greater than 0")

        batches = []
        current_batch = []
        current_rows = 0

        for partition, row_count in items:
            # If the batch is not empty and adding this item exceeds the limit,
            # we must start a new batch.
            if current_batch and current_rows + row_count > max_rows:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_rows = 0

            current_batch.append((partition, row_count))
            current_rows += row_count

        # Append the final batch if it contains items
        if current_batch:
            batches.append(current_batch)

        return batches
    ```

```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_rows = 0

    for partition, row_count in items:
        item = (partition, row_count)

        # If this single item exceeds max_rows, it must be alone
        if row_count > max_rows:
            # Flush current batch if non-empty
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_rows = 0
            # Add the oversized item as its own batch
            batches.append([item])
        else:
            # Check if adding this item would exceed max_rows
            if current_rows + row_count > max_rows:
                # Start a new batch
                if current_batch:
                    batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = [item]
                current_rows = row_count
            else:
                # Add to current batch
                current_batch.append(item)
                current_rows += row_count

    # Don't forget the last batch
    if current_batch:
        batches.append(current_batch)

    return batches
```

```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_total = 0

    for item in items:
        row_count = item[1]

        if row_count > max_rows:
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            batches.append([item])
        elif current_total + row_count > max_rows:
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            current_batch.append(item)
            current_total = row_count
        else:
            current_batch.append(item)
            current_total += row_count

    if current_batch:
        batches.append(current_batch)

    return batches
```
reasoning
Let me think through this problem carefully.

We need to implement `bounded_batches(items, max_rows)` where:
- `items` is an iterable of `(partition, row_count)` tuples
- We need to preserve order
- Return a list of batches, each batch is a list of items
- The total rows in each batch must not exceed `max_rows`
- An item larger than `max_rows` must be alone in its batch
- Reject `max_rows <= 0` (raise an error)
- Consume the iterable once (so we need to convert to a list or iterate through it once)

Let me think about the algorithm:
1. Validate `max_rows > 0`, raise ValueError if not
2. Iterate through items, accumulating them into the current batch
3. For each item, check if adding it would exceed `max_rows`
   - If the item's row_count > max_rows, it must be alone → flush current batch, start new batch with just this item
   - If current batch total + item's row_count > max_rows, flush current batch, start new batch with this item
   - Otherwise, add to current batch
4. At the end, flush the remaining batch

Let me think about edge cases:
- Empty iterable → return empty list
- Item with row_count == max_rows → fits exactly, can be in a batch by itself or with zero-row items
- Item with row_count > max_rows → must be alone (its own batch, even though it exceeds max_rows)
- Items with row_count == 0 → can always be added to any batch

Let me code this up:

```python
def bounded_batches(items, max_rows):
    if max_rows <= 0:
        raise ValueError("max_rows must be positive")

    batches = []
    current_batch = []
    current_total = 0

    for item in items:
        row_count = item[1]

        if row_count > max_rows:
            # Item larger than max_rows must be alone
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            batches.append([item])
        elif current_total + row_count > max_rows:
            # Would exceed max_rows, flush current batch
            if current_batch:
                batches.append(current_batch)
                current_batch = []
                current_total = 0
            current_batch.append(item)
            current_total = row_count
        else:
            current_batch.append(item)
            current_total += row_count

    if current_batch:
        batches.append(current_batch)

    return batches
```

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem says "whose total rows does not exceed max_rows". But then it says "An item larger than max_rows must be alone." So an item larger than max_rows gets its own batch even though that batch's total exceeds max_rows. That's the exception.

Let me also consider: what if current_batch is empty and we have a normal item? Then current_total is 0, so 0 + row_count <= max_rows (since row_count <= max_rows in that branch), so we just add it. That's fine.

What about the case where current_batch is empty and we're in the `elif` branch? That can't happen because if current_batch is empty, current_total is 0, and 0 + row_count > max_rows would mean row_count > max_rows, which is handled by the first branch.

Actually wait - what if row_count == max_rows exactly? Then 0 + max_rows > max_rows is False, so it goes to the else branch and gets added. Good.

What if we have a batch with total = max_rows - 5, and next item has row_count = 5? Then (max_rows - 5) + 5 = max_rows, which is not > max_rows, so it gets added. Good.

I think the solution is correct. Let me also make sure we only consume the iterable once - we iterate through it with a single for loop, so that's fine.

One more thought: should I use `raise ValueError` or some other exception? ValueError seems most appropriate for an invalid argument value.

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    """
    Merge overlapping or touching ranges only when payloads are equal.

    Args:
        items: List of tuples (start, end, payload)

    Returns:
        List of lists [start, end, payload] representing merged ranges

    Raises:
        ValueError if any item has start > end
    """
    # Validate input and reject start > end
    for start, end, payload in items:
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start ({start}) > end ({end})")

    if not items:
        return []

    # Create a copy to avoid mutating input
    # Sort by start, then by end, then by payload
    sorted_items = sorted(items, key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1], x[2]))

    merged = []

    for start, end, payload in sorted_items:
        if not merged:
            merged.append([start, end, payload])
        else:
            last = merged[-1]
            last_start, last_end, last_payload = last

            # Check if current range overlaps or touches with the last merged range
            # Touching means current start <= last end + 1?
            # Actually, "touching" typically means adjacent: current start == last end + 1 for integer ranges
            # But more generally, overlapping or touching means current start <= last end
            # Wait, let's think about this carefully.
            # Two ranges [a,b] and [c,d] overlap if c <= b and a <= d.
            # They touch if they are adjacent, e.g., [1,2] and [3,4] don't touch in continuous sense but might in discrete.
            # The problem says "overlapping or touching". Let's interpret as: current start <= last end + 1 for integer coordinates,
            # or more precisely, the ranges share a boundary point. For continuous ranges, [1,2] and [2,3] touch at point 2.
            # So condition is: current start <= last end (for overlap) or current start == last end (for touching at endpoint).
            # Actually, standard interpretation: two intervals [a,b] and [c,d] with a<=b, c<=d overlap if max(a,c) <= min(b,d).
            # They touch if one ends where the other begins: b == c or d == a.
            # Combined: they can be merged if current start <= last end (overlap) or current start == last end + something?
            # Let me reconsider: "overlapping or touching" usually means the union is connected.
            # For real numbers: [a,b] and [c,d] have connected union iff c <= b (assuming a<=c after sorting by start).
            # Because if c <= b, then [a,b] U [c,d] = [a, max(b,d)] which is connected.
            # If c > b, there's a gap between b and c.

            # So the condition for merging is: current start <= last end

            if start <= last_end:
                # Ranges overlap or touch
                if payload == last_payload:
                    # Merge them
                    new_end = max(last_end, end)
                    last[1] = new_end
                else:
                    # Different payloads, cannot merge
                    merged.append([start, end, payload])
            else:
                # No overlap or touching, add as new range
                merged.append([start, end, payload])

    return merged
```

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    """
    Merge overlapping or touching ranges only when payloads are equal.

    Args:
        items: list of (start, end, payload) tuples

    Returns:
        List of [start, end, payload] lists representing merged ranges.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If any item has start > end.
    """
    # Validate input
    for item in items:
        if len(item) != 3:
            raise ValueError("Each item must be a tuple of (start, end, payload)")
        start, end, payload = item
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start ({start}) > end ({end})")

    # Do not mutate input; work with a copy
    # Sort by start, then by end
    sorted_items = sorted([(s, e, p) for s, e, p in items], key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

    # But merging only happens when payloads are equal.

    # When we encounter a new item, check if it overlaps/touches with existing merged ranges that have the same payload.

    # 1. They have the same payload

    # "Touching" typically means the end of one equals the start of another, so they are adjacent.
    # Overlapping means they share some points.

    # Let's assume touching means c <= b + 1 for discrete, but more generally c <= b + epsilon.
    # For simplicity, let's say they can be merged if next_start <= current_end + 1 (for integers) or next_start <= current_end (for reals).

    # Standard interval merging: intervals [a,b] and [c,d] can be merged if c <= b + 1 (for inclusive integer intervals where [a,b] includes both a and b). But if we're dealing with real numbers, touching would mean c == b.
    # Let's assume the ranges are such that [start, end] is inclusive. Two ranges [a,b] and [c,d] overlap if max(a,c) <= min(b,d). They touch if b == c or d == a.

    # 1. Sort by start, then end.
    # 2. Use a list to hold merged results.
    # 3. For each item, check against the last few merged ranges (or all) with the same payload.

    # Since merging is transitive within the same payload, we can group by payload and merge within each group.

    from collections import defaultdict

    groups = defaultdict(list)
    for s, e, p in sorted_items:
        groups[p].append((s, e))

    result = []
    for payload, ranges in groups.items():
        # Sort ranges by start, then end (already sorted overall, but let's ensure)
        ranges.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

        if not ranges:
            continue

        current_start, current_end = ranges[0]

        for i in range(1, len(ranges)):
            s, e = ranges[i]
            # Check if this range overlaps or touches the current merged range
            # Touching: s <= current_end + 1 (for integer-like) or s <= current_end
            # I'll use s <= current_end + 1 to be safe
            if s <= current_end + 1:
                current_end = max(current_end, e)
            else:
                result.append([current_start, current_end, payload])
                current_start, current_end = s, e

        result.append([current_start, current_end, payload])

    # Sort final result by start, then end
    result.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

    return result
```

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    for start, end, payload in items:
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start {start} > end {end}")

    groups = {}
    for start, end, payload in items:
        groups.setdefault(payload, []).append((start, end))

    result = []
    for payload, intervals in groups.items():
        intervals.sort()
        merged = []
        for start, end in intervals:
            if merged and start <= merged[-1][1]:
                merged[-1] = (merged[-1][0], max(merged[-1][1], end))
            else:
                merged.append((start, end))
        for start, end in merged:
            result.append([start, end, payload])

    result.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))
    return result
```

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    # Validate
    for start, end, payload in items:
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start {start} > end {end}")

    # Sort by start, then by end
    sorted_items = sorted(items, key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))

    # Merge
    result = []
    for item in sorted_items:
        start, end, payload = item
        if result and payload == result[-1][2] and start <= result[-1][1]:
            # Merge: extend the end
            result[-1][1] = max(result[-1][1], end)
        else:
            result.append([start, end, payload])

    return result
```

Option 2 seems cleaner:
1. Group items by payload
2. For each group, sort by start and merge overlapping/touching intervals
3. Combine all results and sort by start

```python
def merge_payload_ranges(items):
    # Validate
    for start, end, payload in items:
        if start > end:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid range: start {start} > end {end}")

    # Group by payload
    from collections import defaultdict
    groups = defaultdict(list)
    for start, end, payload in items:
        groups[payload].append((start, end))

    # Merge within each group
    result = []
    for payload, intervals in groups.items():
        intervals.sort()
        merged = []
        for start, end in intervals:
            if merged and start <= merged[-1][1]:
                merged[-1] = (merged[-1][0], max(merged[-1][1], end))
            else:
                merged.append((start, end))
        for start, end in merged:
            result.append([start, end, payload])

    # Sort final result by start, then end
    result.sort(key=lambda x: (x[0], x[1]))
    return result
```

This is O(n log n) and handles all cases correctly.

Qwen3.6 FP8 · non-thinking

```python
def committable_windows(events, watermark):
    """
    Process events and return a dict mapping window to list of values.

    Args:
        events: iterable of dicts with keys 'window', 'event_time', 'value'
        watermark: threshold for dropping late events

    Returns:
        dict mapping window to list of values (in input order), with windows
        appearing in first-seen order. Late events (event_time <= watermark) are dropped.
    """
    result = {}
    # Track insertion order using a list of unique windows
    window_order = []

    for event in events:
        event_time = event['event_time']
        # Drop late events: event_time <= watermark is late
        if event_time <= watermark:
            continue

        window = event['window']
        value = event['value']

        if window not in result:
            result[window] = []
            window_order.append(window)

        result[window].append(value)

    # Build ordered dict preserving first-seen order
    from collections import OrderedDict
    ordered_result = OrderedDict()
    for window in window_order:
        ordered_result[window] = result[window]

    return ordered_result
```

```python
def committable_windows(events, watermark):
    result = {}
    for event in events:
        if event['event_time'] <= watermark:
            continue
        window = event['window']
        if window not in result:
            result[window] = []
        result[window].append(event['value'])
    return result
```

```python
def committable_windows(events, watermark):
    result = {}
    for event in events:
        if event['event_time'] <= watermark:
            continue
        window = event['window']
        if window not in result:
            result[window] = []
        result[window].append(event['value'])
    return result
```
reasoning
Let me break down the requirements:

1. `def committable_windows(events, watermark)`
2. `events` is an iterable of dicts with keys `window`, `event_time`, `value`
3. Drop late events where `event_time <= watermark`
4. For remaining events, return a dict mapping `window` to values in input order
5. Window keys should be inserted in first-seen order
6. Consume the iterable once (so I can't iterate multiple times)
7. Do not mutate `events`

Let me think about the return value:
- It's a dict mapping `window` to values in input order
- So for each window, the values are collected in the order they appear in the input
- The dict itself should have keys in first-seen order (Python 3.7+ dicts maintain insertion order, so this is natural)

So the structure would be: `{window: [value1, value2, ...], ...}` where the list of values is in input order, and the window keys are in the order they were first encountered.

Let me write this:

```python
def committable_windows(events, watermark):
    result = {}
    for event in events:
        if event['event_time'] <= watermark:
            continue
        window = event['window']
        if window not in result:
            result[window] = []
        result[window].append(event['value'])
    return result
```

This:
- Consumes the iterable once (single for loop)
- Does not mutate `events` (just reads from it)
- Drops late events (`event_time <= watermark`)
- Returns a dict mapping window to list of values in input order
- Window keys are inserted in first-seen order (Python 3.7+ dict behavior)

This looks correct and clean.

- EV1: GPU memory was insufficient, so 9.5 GiB of layers were moved to CPU (this is a CPU offload mechanism)
- EV2: Container RSS (Resident Set Size) rose from 7 GiB to 61 GiB (a 54 GiB increase, consistent with the offloaded layers plus overhead)
- EV3: Kernel invoked OOM killer (the system ran out of RAM)
- EV4: GPU had another process using 18 GiB (explaining why GPU memory was insufficient)

Root cause analysis:
- The chain of events: Another process was using 18 GiB on GPU (EV4) → GPU memory was insufficient (EV1) → 9.5 GiB layers were offloaded to CPU (EV1) → Container memory ballooned from 7 to 61 GiB (EV2) → OOM killer was invoked (EV3)
- The root cause is `gpu_to_cpu_offload` — the offloading mechanism triggered a memory cascade that led to OOM.

Action codes (choose exactly two highest priority):
- `disable_cpu_offload` — This directly addresses the root cause. If we disable the offload, the system would fail fast on GPU OOM rather than cascading into CPU RAM exhaustion.
- `vram_guard` — This would prevent the situation by monitoring GPU memory and handling the other process (EV4) or preventing the offload when GPU is occupied by other processes.
- `container_memory_limit` — This would contain the damage but not prevent it.
- `increase_swap` — This is a band-aid.

The two highest priority actions:
1. `disable_cpu_offload` — directly addresses the root cause mechanism
2. `vram_guard` — prevents the triggering condition (GPU occupied by other process) from causing the offload

These two together prevent both the root cause and the triggering condition.

- `database_cpu_saturation`: CPU is only 28% — ruled out.
- `lock_contention`: Locks=0 — ruled out.

Action codes (choose exactly two highest priority):
- `bounded_pool_increase`: The pool is saturated at max=40. Increasing the pool size (in a bounded way) is the most direct fix.
- `observe_wait_p95`: Monitoring the wait time is important to track the effect of changes, but it's lower priority than actually fixing the problem.
- `statement_timeout`: Adding a statement timeout could help prevent long-running queries from holding connections, but the evidence doesn't suggest slow queries (DB CPU is low, no locks).
- `disable_timeout`: This would be harmful — disabling timeouts is generally a bad practice.

The two highest-priority actions:
1. `bounded_pool_increase` — directly addresses the root cause (pool saturation).
2. `observe_wait_p95` — after increasing the pool, you need to monitor to confirm the wait time decreases.

Actually, let me reconsider. `statement_timeout` could be a secondary action to ensure that if individual queries are holding connections too long, they get released. But the evidence shows DB CPU is low and no locks, suggesting queries aren't slow — it's just that there aren't enough connections to handle the concurrency. So `bounded_pool_increase` is the primary fix, and `observe_wait_p95` is the monitoring action to validate.

I'll go with `bounded_pool_increase` and `observe_wait_p95`.
